## Part opening

<!-- pedagogy-unit: part:OBSERVATIONS::part-opening -->

Part ID: `part:OBSERVATIONS`

Purpose:

> The next studies connect neural activity to eye control in monkeys and to location in rodents and bats. Chapters 7–9 follow the path from a neural command through eye muscles to measured movement, and test a possible source of drift. Chapters 10–12 explain where hippocampal nerve cells fire as an animal moves and compare a spatial model with recordings. Chapter 13 asks what these findings establish together and which connections remain untested.

## ch:EYE-DRIFT - Eye drift points to a shared neural drive

Source file: `content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md`

Purpose: Even when a monkey looks at a stationary spot, its eyes drift slightly. This chapter explains how paired measurements of eye angle and the firing of neurons that command eye muscles support a shared neural source of that drift, before considering a particular circuit mechanism.

Prerequisites: ch:PERSISTENCE, ch:MEMORY-DYNAMICS

Sidecar sections: From stored eye position to muscle action, The recordings pair one motor neuron with movement at a time, A fitted model links movement to firing rate, A calibrated filter predicts eye position from one cell's spikes, The measured correlation challenges independent motor noise alone, Calibrated covariance estimates the common share of movement variance

Blocks: 6 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md::ch:EYE-DRIFT -->

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p3 sha256 2a5966598c907783ee4063f8dcad08e49e5697d2953c4277abcfb3bcb4134dd2 -->
p3, lines 3-5; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: none; next: p7

> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = NO --> Looking steadily at a small spot does not hold the eye perfectly still. [Between brief, rapid movements called [**microsaccades**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, the eye makes a small, smooth, irregular movement called fixational drift.]{.definition-explanation} [Does this movement begin as noise in the muscles, or does the brain send a changing command to them?]{.key-statement} Ben-Shushan and colleagues answer this in two stages. Their recordings support a large shared drive upstream of the motor neurons. Their model then proposes a particular source: random motion of the eye position stored in a memory circuit.[^p2-question] <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p7 sha256 ca0bc9dead0d4e995e3e496d098a0a1213854eccf928570a5bf5ff1473f4f55a -->
p7, lines 7-7; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p3; next: p9

> This distinction connects the previous chapters to a measurement outside the brain. A memory coordinate can diffuse inside a neural network, but the movement produced by that coordinate also depends on the muscles and the mechanics of the eye. We need to follow the signal through those stages before comparing a neural diffusion law with an eye trajectory.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p9 sha256 3240811d7c0d01b6d80c1c03bc1ac671c9735238e431fb6625d93370d9d8c698 -->
p9, lines 9-9; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p7; next: p13

> A recording from one motor neuron becomes useful for this comparison only after its spikes and the eye's movement can be expressed on the same scale. The larger movements provide that calibration. Applying it during fixation then lets us ask whether independent motor noise could explain the observed relationship, and estimate the shared contribution under explicit gain and independence assumptions.

### From stored eye position to muscle action

Location: line 11, anchor `sec:EYE-PATHWAY`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 13
Closing passage line: 36

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p13 sha256 0129b6c4252cd6d9a0fe9575bd864abf95d9ba35480c2d0d2180a289f768c45a -->
p13, lines 13-15; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p9; next: p17

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> The eye needs an ongoing command to maintain a position. A brief command to move is therefore insufficient: after the movement, a circuit must maintain activity representing the new position. [The [**oculomotor integrator**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is the neural circuit that performs this storage. “Integrator” refers to turning a movement command into a lasting position signal.]{.definition-explanation} In the proposed mechanism, the continuous attractor described in the [foundations](ch-persistence.html#sec-foundations-attractor) and [network model](ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-spikes) stores horizontal eye position. Noise moves the stored value along that attractor.[^p2-pathway] <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p17 sha256 9e6c0bae0b900651eb53b64677fbb3bc3e9e4c5e6bc5fa124b8776fe0c500798 -->
p17, lines 17-19; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p13; next: p21

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeCarries = NO --> [The signal reaches [**ocular motoneurons (OMNs)**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, neurons whose spikes activate the eye muscles.]{.definition-explanation} [The recordings came from the [**abducens nucleus**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, a group of brainstem neurons that includes OMNs activating the lateral rectus muscle, which turns the eye outward.]{.definition-explanation} Other neurons in that nucleus carry signals to other neurons rather than directly to muscle. The study separated these cell types by how their firing changed with eye position and movement. [Finally, the muscles and the mechanical properties of the eye, together called the [**ocular plant**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, turn neural activity into movement.]{.definition-explanation}[^p2-recordings] <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeCarries = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p21 sha256 a24a2319d48bd386c94e7b85be54277c1ed845ddc8bd86d17b98b23ea85b6a20 -->
p21, lines 21-23; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p17; next: p27

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md::sec:EYE-RECORDINGS -->

### The recordings pair one motor neuron with movement at a time

Location: line 44, anchor `sec:EYE-RECORDINGS`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 46
Closing passage line: 73

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p46 sha256 8ec35faccc51c610e89204215d679707d2e2517cbee7acd5d594856998f38c29 -->
p46, lines 46-48; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p36; next: p50

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> The authors reanalyzed earlier experiments in two male rhesus macaques. With their heads held still, the monkeys first looked at a stationary spot of light on a monitor and then followed the moving spot. A wire search coil on one eye measured the eye's angle while an electrode recorded one neuron's spikes. There were 57 identified OMNs. [Each recording paired one OMN with eye movement. There were no simultaneous recordings of multiple OMNs. Thus the analysis cannot directly measure the covariance between two recorded motor neurons.]{.key-statement}[^p2-recordings][^p2-single] <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p50 sha256 e17b7a73adb85760213e6b0f77e18d22d6991fd03555fe389dcdab6491e8a9b1 -->
p50, lines 50-54; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p46; next: p56

> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO --> The initial stationary target was followed by 500–700 ms of required fixation before the target stepped and began moving. Neural voltage was sampled at 25 kHz and eye position at 1 kHz. The authors detected microsaccades using eye speed and acceleration, removed 30 ms on each side of them, and manually checked the remaining segments. For the main displacement analysis, segments shorter than 350 ms were excluded and longer ones were split into nonoverlapping 350 ms pieces. There were 302 such pieces per cell on average, with different counts across cells. These repeated observations improve estimates within two animals. They do not create 57 independent animal samples.[^p2-segments] <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p56 sha256 715a3b40870e606d42e18c6be920744cf67fde4198516b64afa3596f75d3d8c5 -->
p56, lines 56-58; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p50; next: p62

> <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure"> <summary>Original paper figure: Ben-Shushan et al. (2022), Extended Data Figure 7</summary> <div class="publisher-fold-body">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p62 sha256 fcd1e43c0c40c2d1dd2932edba0594c38927752553522ce5ed959562b6a78f49 -->
p62, lines 62-63; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p56; next: p65

> </div> </details>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p65 sha256 b140aa98eb75347476a66f90ab2bae3cccf4d04c0697d462871f425ac57ba4f4 -->
p65, lines 65-65; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p62; next: p69

> The large movements have a useful role even though the scientific question concerns tiny drifts. They produce enough rate change to estimate how each cell's activity depends on eye movement. The fitted relationship is then applied during fixation. This transfer is an assumption to examine, because an incorrect gain during drift would change the inferred common variance. Gain is the scale that converts a change in one signal into a change in the other.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p69 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p69, lines 69-69; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p65; next: p73

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md::sec:EYE-FORWARD-FIT -->

### A fitted model links movement to firing rate

Location: line 77, anchor `sec:EYE-FORWARD-FIT`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 79
Closing passage line: 125

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p79 sha256 a025000845c2b9ae48adab129a44b434db901193bc8ab78dc71dc1019d4574f5 -->
p79, lines 79-81; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p73; next: p88

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> Position alone does not predict a motor neuron's activity during a movement. At the same position, firing can differ when the eye is moving or accelerating. The paper therefore fits <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p88 sha256 35fbef1321d69097d5535fe106bd53ca030f8ada3646621fe938d6269e80e804 -->
p88, lines 88-88; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p79; next: p96

> Here $E$ is horizontal eye position in degrees and $\widehat f$ is predicted firing rate in spikes/s. The position threshold $E_T$ sets where the position term reaches zero. The coefficients have units

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p96 sha256 b52ef5bb23ab58b6ff244096877ca8c54f7bddf411f43ec8fcfa4a81709db446 -->
p96, lines 96-96; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p88; next: p98

> Multiplying by position, velocity, and acceleration gives spikes/s in all three terms.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p98 sha256 60a09cfbfc8dea17dc23823672cd9deeddea3c0d79fd0b70180494722a05c164 -->
p98, lines 98-100; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p96; next: p102

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> The position term describes ongoing firing associated with holding the eye at an angle. The velocity term allows a different rate while the eye passes through that same angle. The acceleration term allows another change while its velocity changes. Thus $k$, $r$, and $m$ describe different demands on the motor signal. They are fitted coefficients of the combined neural and mechanical relationship. In particular, $m$ is not a direct measurement of the eye's mass. The delay $\Delta t$ is in seconds: firing at time $t$ predicts movement at the later time $t+\Delta t$ for a positive neural lead. This is the paper's Eq. 2, including its threshold and time shift.[^p2-forward] <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p102 sha256 f3fc314e12bf31a6fd59e7b34dfb99e911184c925b34e79a5945cd1023947062 -->
p102, lines 102-102; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p98; next: p109

> The observed rate $f(t)$ was estimated as the inverse of the interval between successive spikes, constant between those spikes. For each cell, the authors minimized the sum of squared rate errors,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p109 sha256 c36d17f381179b57d22c89b867c01ec25faa88683b6c1562b24179d0ee188176 -->
p109, lines 109-109; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p102; next: p111

> $a$ labels trials and $T_a$ is trial duration. This is Eq. 3 with the trial count renamed so that it cannot be confused with a neuron count. Fits used full trials, including fixation and pursuit. To predict a trial's rate, that trial was left out of the parameter fit. Acceleration was estimated from velocity after local polynomial smoothing over 21 ms. Trials in which the rate fell below 20 Hz were discarded: the linear formula is intended for active cells above firing threshold, whereas firing cannot become negative.[^p2-forward]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p111 sha256 4570709d356f2fc809101ed7d649ddb6ecacf5de27c998d9f4460bff263dcef5 -->
p111, lines 111-117; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p109; next: p119

> <!-- vale Google.Units = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO --> During fixation, the authors subtracted each segment's mean firing rate, joined the segments, and compared observed and predicted rate fluctuations. Figure 1c illustrates a weak relationship for one cell. Fig. 1d shows a positive population effect. The one sided test asks whether the mean correlation is positive, compared with a zero-mean reference. [Its [**p value**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is the probability, under that reference, of a test statistic at least as large as observed.]{.definition-explanation} Across 57 cells it gave $p=3\times10^{-9}$. A weak single cell relationship is expected here because the tiny movement produces small rate changes against much larger spike variability. This is the first evidence of a relationship, before any estimate of the fraction of common motion.[^p2-rate-result] <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES --> <!-- vale Google.Units = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p119 sha256 7bdb311511f99b82f006e27389a713c838c70b56d25ed0e0a9c1d8ec53d11e76 -->
p119, lines 119-121; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p111; next: p125

> <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure"> <summary>Original paper figure: Ben-Shushan et al. (2022), Figure 1</summary> <div class="publisher-fold-body">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p125 sha256 fcd1e43c0c40c2d1dd2932edba0594c38927752553522ce5ed959562b6a78f49 -->
p125, lines 125-126; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p119; next: p130

> </div> </details>

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md::sec:EYE-INVERSE-FILTER -->

### A calibrated filter predicts eye position from one cell's spikes

Location: line 128, anchor `sec:EYE-INVERSE-FILTER`

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p130 sha256 700a56c69375b0cf2623d99532c897bb8144a0377048422a6e0cc58aa679579f -->
p130, lines 130-134; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p125; next: p136

> <!-- vale ai-tells.ServesAsDodge = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = NO --> To ask how much eye movement is shared with a neuron, it helps to express both signals in degrees. [The paper reverses the fitted relationship: a cell's spike train becomes the input, and its prediction of eye position becomes the output. This operation is an inverse of the movement-to-rate fit, rather than a new fit to small drift residuals.]{.key-statement} <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.ServesAsDodge = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p136 sha256 4f6c7d464c5f055b961eb1cbfceab1493dc76af31b519d8f4014631f024aa40f -->

> Let $x(t)=E(t)-E_T$. Ignoring the time shift for a moment, the deterministic relation is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p142 sha256 9da3178d07bcaa8ee393972de4b72981935dfa415ef937d7366c71bca3d76f36 -->
p142, lines 142-142; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p136; next: p149

> [A [**causal filter**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} computes its present output from present and past input. For the equation above, its [**impulse response**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} $h_0(u)$ is the position response to one instantaneous spike at time zero.]{.definition-explanation} To factor the equation, consider a time component $e^{pt}$: differentiation multiplies it by $p$. The algebraic ratio of output to input for that component is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p149 sha256 19dbd09a54232a01ba1f66b8e50cd5c1f1d56443af3ae6383d94723a2d4a446f -->
p149, lines 149-149; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p142; next: p156

> Factoring the quadratic gives the slow and fast decay times

p156, lines 156-156; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p149; next: p158

> These are the paired signs in the paper's Eq. 7, with $\tau_+>\tau_-$ when $k,r,m>0$ and $r^2>4km$. Under these conditions both components decay without oscillation.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p158 sha256 3248b6d2744bf05c8ea246a86fe27cbe19876d1e959b6c31a3ae60fc06fa5bfd -->
p158, lines 158-158; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p156; next: p165

> At rest, the inverse equation gives $x=f/k$: with the threshold fixed, doubling $k$ halves the position offset predicted from the same steady firing rate. During a change, $r$ and $m$ also affect the response through the two timescales. This is a consequence of the fitted relationship, not evidence that changing one recorded neuron's rate alone sets eye position. The same factorization gives two useful identities:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p165 sha256 a2ac4d749ee4165fb46a31c375d552a55ed758a66b963b8690e4d5da22e3b600 -->
p165, lines 165-165; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p158; next: p173

> The corresponding impulse response is

p173, lines 173-173; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p165; next: p180

> $\Theta(u)$ is zero for $u<0$ and one for $u>0$, which makes the response zero before the input. Restoring the fitted neural lead delays this response: $h(u)=h_0(u-\Delta t)$. Write the spike train as $\xi(t)=\sum_j\delta(t-t_j)$, where $\delta$ is an ideal instantaneous impulse of unit area, so integration over one spike counts one event. The prediction is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p180 sha256 81bf2e49cf8f72f7aefc3a17ecb8eb51263b320934a5cb1dc4b963735fa941ce -->
p180, lines 180-180; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p173; next: p184

> These expressions reproduce Eqs. 4–6, including the factor $\tau_+\tau_-/m$ and the delay. [The [**convolution**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} integral adds the responses to all past spikes.]{.definition-explanation} With spike count treated as a count, $\xi$ has units spikes/s and $h$ has units degrees/spike. Each spike contributes a decaying position response. Adding those responses produces degrees.[^p2-inversion]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p184 sha256 4eec0db35e0cd5097e595acded14851f798b0df8940c381d3a89cfa31f16d34f -->
p184, lines 184-184; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p180; next: p190

> We can obtain the response to a spike directly from the differential equation. After the spike, the input is zero, and each of the two exponentials solves the remaining homogeneous equation. Write their combination as

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p190 sha256 2b11c348121aaf68f36e86b724d32e54d5d0c5a6b5e40011ffd4977e91bdd33a -->
p190, lines 190-194; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p184; next: p201

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> To find $A$ and $B$, treat the spike as a short pulse of unit area and shrink its duration to zero. The position change during that pulse tends to zero, so $h_0(0^+)=0$. Integrating $m h_0''+r h_0'+k h_0$ across the pulse gives $m h_0'(0^+)=1$: the velocity changes by $1/m$, while position and velocity before the causal response are zero. Therefore <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p201 sha256 b14ab3ab01aa24c73cb408fb20e8bd0cd9d57dfec6e5a63b7b84e98076477cb0 -->
p201, lines 201-203; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p190; next: p207

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> Substituting $B=-A$ gives $A(1/\tau_- -1/\tau_+)=1/m$, hence $A=\tau_+\tau_-/[m(\tau_+-\tau_-)]$. Making the response zero before the spike gives the impulse response $h_0(u)$. <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p207 sha256 9b2ee830691f3039bde3143aa4ee58dbc946248e991762c153bd2baf666366b1 -->
p207, lines 207-207; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p201; next: p214

> For a constructed example, take $k=4$ spikes/(s deg), $\tau_+=0.120$ s, and $\tau_-=0.005$ s. The identities above require $r=0.500$ spikes/deg and $m=0.00240$ spikes s/deg. Then

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p214 sha256 482bed9757d0fbfcfcd37997f610c1ef876715486fcd700a313a5b0479c274a0 -->
p214, lines 214-214; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p207; next: p218

> and its area is $2.174(0.120-0.005)=0.250$ deg s/spike, equal to $1/k$. A steady rate of 80 spikes/s predicts an offset of 20 degrees above $E_T$. This checks the gain and the time constants.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p218 sha256 1682b05d154bb130d709606004cc56a1752f384ec76339a5c9586211f2f1c302 -->
p218, lines 218-218; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p214; next: p226

> The kernel's area checks its normalization:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p226 sha256 4e59a4348c15a1581516a6c8ca969102bf861f59e842ff5a910028e12aa8c778 -->
p226, lines 226-228; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p218; next: p232

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> Thus a constant input rate $f_0$ predicts $E_T+f_0/k$, exactly the static forward relation. A kernel with unit area would give the wrong conversion unless $k$ had already been absorbed into the input. When the two decay times coincide, $r^2=4km$, the limit is $h_0(u)=\Theta(u)u e^{-u/\tau}/m$. The distinct exponential formula is used for the fitted responses in the paper. <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p232 sha256 5ab2abd8beef627d73db6c427d9aa52921b8462b467a0c4e69ad4cfc231381e4 -->
p232, lines 232-238; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p226; next: p240

> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = NO --> Figure 2a–b shows typical fitted times of about 5 ms and 120 ms. Figure 2c shows that a single cell's reconstructed drift is much noisier than the measured movement. Across 350 ms displacement pairs, the mean Pearson correlation was $\langle R\rangle=0.17\pm0.02$, [where the error is the [**standard error of the mean (SEM)**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}. SEM estimates uncertainty in the across-cell mean, rather than the spread of individual cells or uncertainty across animals.]{.definition-explanation} The one sided test gave $p=3.8\times10^{-12}$. Shuffling which measured segment was paired with which spike prediction centered the comparison near zero. Here and below $\langle\cdot\rangle$ over cells means a population average.[^p2-correlation] <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

### The measured correlation challenges independent motor noise alone

Location: line 249, anchor `sec:EYE-SHARED-VARIANCE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 251
Closing passage line: 289

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p251 sha256 b54158d1b0264f8858ec0971143edd70bb5633c70c4b329e7ea088dd725c42bf -->
p251, lines 251-251; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p246; next: p253

> A correlation of 0.17 does not immediately tell us how much motion comes from the common command. First we can ask a more limited question: could all the motion come from independent motor neuron fluctuations?

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p253 sha256 7b91bf7f7698525cd5c8f1eb6f2895f170f6d4d45c4d9a54f438660a098f924f -->
p253, lines 253-253; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p251; next: p261

> For a centered displacement $Y$ and one centered prediction $X$, the best linear prediction $bX$ minimizes $\operatorname{Var}(Y-bX)$. Differentiating with respect to $b$ gives $b=\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)/\operatorname{Var}(X)$. Its explained fraction of variance is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p261 sha256 b59eaa31a873e36c591d28219fb63b3016401079cd2a68fc5699df4f031dc850 -->
p261, lines 261-261; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p253; next: p263

> [This is the [**coefficient of determination**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} for a linear prediction with a fitted gain.]{.definition-explanation} It does not assert that the raw, unscaled position predictor itself has a small squared error.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p263 sha256 19e045d8607cf3876e73fdbde10520406a5e677e00c0c12c4b2e1857a8d6b503 -->
p263, lines 263-263; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p261; next: p271

> Suppose there are $N_m$ independent single neuron predictors $X_i$. Standardize each to variance one and also standardize $Y$. Independence makes the $X_i$ uncorrelated, so the residual after using them all has variance

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p271 sha256 84c3bc00e9a6a82566e47b7e4179cfd7735f8c61ddb2bfe0898a85bc6e2fdf11 -->
p271, lines 271-273; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p263; next: p275

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> Consequently $\sum_iR_i^2\le1$, and an average neuron explains at most $1/N_m$. This proves the bound used in Supplementary Eq. S1. It does not require equal muscle effects from all neurons. Applying it to the sampled cells does require their average explanatory power to represent the relevant motor population.[^p2-independent-bound] <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p275 sha256 0514e747eaf71b383c2e16919c381e3a8ef595ae2542aeaa531c338157ab6990 -->
p275, lines 275-275; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p271; next: p283

> The authors avoid directly averaging noisy squared correlations. Instead,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p283 sha256 ec63b306eb2b2b7d7baa737eb276fa588ed25d10c6702c50f5b72e1b85a621e7 -->
p283, lines 283-287; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p275; next: p289

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> [Thus the estimated mean correlation implies an average explained fraction of at least about 2.9%. For 1,000 independently contributing motor neurons, the bound would be 0.1%. For several thousand it is smaller. The mismatch argues against independent OMN noise as the sole source.]{.key-statement} Shared fluctuations can allow many cells to explain the same part of the movement, so their explained fractions can overlap.[^p2-independent-bound] <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p289 sha256 56567703098d6aaa1d1e145327c006422918468b8966daa0ac8fff9abcc8dcc9 -->
p289, lines 289-289; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p283; next: p293

> This step finds shared variation. Locating its source upstream also uses a biological premise: the private variability generated within OMNs is taken to be independent across cells. The paper supports that premise using anatomical and stimulation studies indicating little local recurrent coupling in the relevant motor nuclei. It was not tested by simultaneous recordings in this data set. Common inputs can correlate the total activity of OMNs even when their privately generated spike noise is independent.[^p2-independence]

Expert objection cannot be marked Not applicable. Write the strongest correction a domain expert would make to this unit's text or figures; set kind to correctness or wording; name its target passage or figure label; set disposition to fixed or does not apply; give the reason. An objection of none is not a review. The strongest correction is the one that would change what a reader believes or does. Aim first at what the unit claims: a promise word such as always, once, ensures or remains that is false or needs a condition; a named method described wrongly; code that does not do what the text says; a table cell; a diagnostic sequence that skips a stage; a verb without an actor; a label naming something other than what is drawn; a figure that disagrees with the text or another figure; and example content found in neither the chapter nor its sources. Claims to check lists the unit's statements most likely to make checkable claims: every table row and every figure or table caption with its alt text, and each sentence with a promise word, a quantity or calculation, code font, a citation, or a section or figure reference. Footnote definitions are left to the citation checks. When packets compare with a revision, every changed statement is listed first and marked changed. The list is a floor, not the extent of the review.
For each code listing, write test: followed by the path, relative to the guide, of a test that runs it on the inputs the text makes claims about, or not run: followed by the reason and how its claims were checked instead.
Essential explanation cannot be marked Not applicable. Trace the reader's task and its dependencies, including gaps not covered by other fields. Record unresolved: none only after resolving essential gaps; otherwise describe them and keep the unit verdict open. A complete checklist is not proof of complete teaching.

### Calibrated covariance estimates the common share of movement variance

Location: line 291, anchor `sec:EYE-CHI`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 293
Closing passage line: 441

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p293 sha256 5b267d9a5441eefaab6eb0da817e3a33d116a2703cb37ff7260bc3e0bd54c5e7 -->
p293, lines 293-295; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p289; next: p302

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = NO --> The variance bound establishes that a shared signal exists, but leaves its size open. The next calculation uses the inverse filter’s calibrated gain in degrees. For one 350 ms interval, write the measured displacement and the single cell prediction as <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p302 sha256 6bc701a23635177bea72d10dc7233663045c89945fa679b325ebbbd3851b0cf6 -->
p302, lines 302-302; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p293; next: p304

> where $C=\delta E_C$ is the common upstream contribution, $\eta$ combines motor neuron, muscle, and measurement contributions to measured displacement, and $\epsilon$ is the prediction error from the individual cell. All are displacement variables in degrees. The calculation assumes:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p304 sha256 fcb0ac72ee191a4f8f325d04fa91ace88b2018a7abea69d0857ca32e76bf5960 -->
p304, lines 304-304; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p302; next: p305

p305, lines 305-306; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p304; next: p307

> The common contribution enters both signals with unit gain, meaning the same one-for-one displacement scale. <!-- vale ai-tells.ConsequenceParticiple = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p307 sha256 1123ff44b5004403cf55fac632778b7b6afac696ea9175b6723c5e4950dabcd0 -->
p307, lines 307-307; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p305; next: p309

> $C,\eta,\epsilon$ are pairwise uncorrelated to the required accuracy.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p309 sha256 356508fc3c8d3669483313f8bfde6762292bb1706be20c7e2101031a89d8dd82 -->
p309, lines 309-309; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p307; next: p318

> Expanding the covariance shows why those assumptions matter:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p318 sha256 8f5e116c5ca551e459db7769a42cce1555f0592e85dbefddc473d11bf5ff3614 -->
p318, lines 318-320; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p309; next: p327

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> The common fraction is therefore estimated by <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p327 sha256 fa0dccc6a37f55f62728761f000ccf545242b050c2e6795366245a86f728d1eb -->
p327, lines 327-327; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p318; next: p329

> These are Eqs. 11–15 in displacement form. The gain assumption requires that the relation calibrated during larger movements still applies during drift. There is also a small covariance between $\epsilon$ and $\eta$, because the recorded neuron's private spikes contribute both to its predictor and to actual eye motion. The paper neglects this contribution as order $1/N_m$ under its many neuron model.[^p2-chi-method]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p329 sha256 01e7757b7ffe43dfc2273cd2895457e71bb691dd44694c85207a8e7d89aa11eb -->
p329, lines 329-329; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p327; next: p337

> Correlation divides the same covariance by a different denominator:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p337 sha256 0c8f50d52233fddaaa2a6534b98cb0afbc096e88762d93eb3fd0d87dd236af82 -->
p337, lines 337-337; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p329; next: p339

> [Large private noise in a cell inflates $\operatorname{Var}(X)$ and makes $R$ small. It need not make the common contribution to the real movement small, because many independent private contributions average down there.]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p339 sha256 8e19185c156210567a045f638d7d3d1269765f40e35d9cff42cebc457898c408 -->
p339, lines 339-339; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p337; next: p341

p341, lines 341-341; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p339; next: p343

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p343 sha256 a841d6d8f69dd90187cf5a9b772724ce86405deae7bbed22c59c460a37c883e3 -->
p343, lines 343-343; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p341; next: p351

> These numbers are constructed to expose the distinction, not extracted from individual cells. Take independent centered variables with

> Then $\operatorname{Var}(Y)=0.01000$, $\operatorname{Var}(X)=0.23266$, and $\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)=0.00820$, all in deg$^2$. It follows that

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p358 sha256 c27b8a3fca0d51e90e4e540d56b4886a58baa5e535a6c2ef2ca3cd298050520d -->
p358, lines 358-360; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p351; next: p364

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = NO --> One cell explains $R^2\simeq2.89\%$ of displacement variance after linear rescaling, while the common signal supplies 82% of actual displacement variance. The larger, poorly predicted part of the single cell signal is private prediction noise. <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = YES -->

p370, lines 370-372; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p368; next: p374

> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO --> The bars use the same physical scale, so the equal common pieces have equal lengths. See [Shared and private variability, Eqs. 11–15, p. 10](../papers/ben-shushan-2022-fixational-drift.pdf#page=10). <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p374 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p374, lines 374-374; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p370; next: p376

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p376 sha256 a65b6fdc44c554175747a64e282a650477b18593ddf8eb1d86a93d5a0618d3a5 -->
p376, lines 376-376; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p374; next: p384

> In the experiment, each cell gives a noisy estimate $\chi_i$, and those estimates can lie outside $[0,1]$. The underlying variance fraction remains in that interval under the model, but the sample covariance need not. The paper estimates each standard error $\Delta\chi_i$ mainly from covariance uncertainty, treating the measured displacement variance in the denominator as comparatively well determined. It then uses inverse variance weighting:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p384 sha256 23a97a29c6b7cc995409d754db42c8e8e35505afc0ce13fd20a63ad1452e108e -->
p384, lines 384-386; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p376; next: p388

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> To see the weighting, let normalized weights $a_i$ sum to one. Independent errors give variance $\sum_i a_i^2(\Delta\chi_i)^2$ for $\sum_i a_i\chi_i$. Minimizing this subject to $\sum_i a_i=1$ gives $a_i\propto1/(\Delta\chi_i)^2$ and the error above. The paper additionally assumes approximately normal errors around a common value. The last expression is a propagated estimation error under those assumptions. It does not measure variation across animals or uncertainty in the gain and independence assumptions. [The reported result was $0.82\pm0.07$, a weighted mean and weighted error for horizontal displacement over 350 ms. It is neither a fraction of distance traveled nor the fraction that one neuron's spikes accurately predict.]{.key-statement}[^p2-chi-error] <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

## ch:EYE-TIMING - Drift growth distinguishes diffusion from filtered white noise

Source file: `content/chapters/04b-eye-timing.md`

Purpose: Eye movements recorded during steady gaze in monkeys look different over short and long time intervals. This chapter uses their timing to distinguish fluctuations around a fixed command from accumulated changes in the command, while accounting for eye-position measurement error.

Prerequisites: ch:EYE-DRIFT

Sidecar sections: Independent measurement error adds a constant offset to the MSD, The MSD plateaus for filtered white noise but grows for filtered diffusion

Blocks: 4 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

p3, lines 3-5; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: none; next: p7

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = NO --> The shared movement estimate concerns a single interval length. [It leaves open whether the command merely fluctuates around a stable value or keeps accumulating changes. Comparing displacements over different time separations can distinguish these possibilities, but the smallest measured displacements may include a large contribution from recording error.]{.key-statement} <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p7 sha256 67f5d8a6a1a82ef6848a40693e7df754d2e0adcd101947315a06c81f7341df5e -->
p7, lines 7-7; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p3; next: p11

> The mean squared displacement measures the average squared position change over a chosen interval. Separating the recording error from that quantity is necessary before interpreting its growth. To test whether the command accumulates changes, we compare two possible inputs after a decaying muscle response: fluctuations that do not accumulate and a position that wanders by diffusion. Their different long interval behavior explains how a smooth eye movement can still reflect a wandering stored value.

Location: line 9, anchor `sec:EYE-MEASUREMENT-NOISE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 11
Closing passage line: 72

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p11 sha256 e26517c7c3b722e57d00556f1730c44e772c7af9401ce0116ca28796a8552480 -->
p11, lines 11-11; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p7; next: p17

> Using the [earlier definition of mean squared displacement](ch-foundations.html#sec-foundations-covariance),

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p17 sha256 61e04c7a4d88eae14f10f9fb9dfbcc2d3fea58e34f6077a8eabf1ac7f5d59b63 -->
p17, lines 17-17; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p11; next: p19

> is the mean squared displacement, or MSD, at lag $\ell$. The data estimate it within each microsaccade free segment, then average the available segment estimates at each lag. Larger lags have fewer eligible segments. The 1 ms sampling sets the available lag spacing.[^p2-msd]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p19 sha256 152949ab575328d32f2822b44acf9df72b8bacd3d378a2aad5fdc3895894c14c -->
p19, lines 19-19; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p17; next: p21

> At short lags, the displacement can be smaller than the search coil's measurement error. Suppose its reading is $E_{\rm obs}(t)=E(t)+\nu(t)$. The measurement error is assumed to have:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p21 sha256 5768505d76ac7e939baaff1c40a7a4c6e00acf5ccebf83fb2bba0287f07732ce -->
p21, lines 21-21; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p19; next: p22

> Zero mean and variance $\sigma_m^2$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p22 sha256 0775085bf03157799ee96fd6888b06928f2c2c3db4a0a5eaabc13d8bc5cc4526 -->
p22, lines 22-22; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p21; next: p23

> Independence from true movement.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p23 sha256 2270208b4d7255eab106fe5a80e3d004d88ac293a6f1d3cb81b78b3a41d98760 -->
p23, lines 23-23; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p22; next: p25

> No correlation between distinct sampled times.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p25 sha256 6e62261cd1b9232e0ef2b260b3c11235b8716d400b8d44e2b1069f6b8e836bdd -->
p25, lines 25-25; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p23; next: p37

> For a positive lag,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p37 sha256 39a68e9a7df202be1c2f377bd2770a210cd9e20b18b793cb590d2455c5ca52c9 -->
p37, lines 37-43; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p25; next: p45

> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = NO --> The mixed movement-error term vanishes by independence. This is Eqs. 18–19. The correction is the variance of a difference of two errors, $2\sigma_m^2$, rather than the variance of a single position error. At exactly zero lag the same error is subtracted from itself, so the MSD is zero. A positive lag plateau near zero lag can still occur when successive measurement errors are independent.[^p2-msd-noise] <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p45 sha256 7ba5b0dcb61582d6d3f1fde42a114bfcf2bec3c7ea6c22ec2348fb295b3e3017 -->
p45, lines 45-47; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p37; next: p49

> <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO --> The authors estimated the plateau contribution at order $10^{-3}$ deg$^2$, checked its scale with a coil fixed in space, and subtracted that contribution from the observed MSD. [This is a correction of an average statistic, not knowledge of each trial's unknown noise.]{.key-statement} Smoothing the eye trajectories instead would also change the movement statistics the analysis seeks to measure. If the plateau estimate has uncertainty $10^{-4}$ deg$^2$, a corrected MSD of that size is unreliable even when the plateau itself is much larger. The peer review response uses this example to explain the shortest lag limitation.[^p2-noise-limit] <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p49 sha256 89627107c32192e51784de1d71a40b67fe0be268a5c7e386a641b13bccb5f57e -->
p49, lines 49-51; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p45; next: p55

> <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure"> <summary>Original paper figure: Ben-Shushan et al. (2022), Extended Data Figure 2</summary> <div class="publisher-fold-body">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p55 sha256 fcd1e43c0c40c2d1dd2932edba0594c38927752553522ce5ed959562b6a78f49 -->
p55, lines 55-56; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p49; next: p58

> </div> </details>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p58 sha256 e89b2f70941cf314fd09f197f09f1dbf7c9aa041955a6d56dae062dda34c4455 -->
p58, lines 58-62; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p55; next: p64

> <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerLeads = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> For instance, a constructed measured MSD of $0.0014$ deg$^2$ with an estimated noise contribution $0.0010$ deg$^2$ leaves $0.0004$ deg$^2$. A $0.0001$ deg$^2$ uncertainty in the subtraction is already 25% of that result. This is why a corrected short lag point needs an uncertainty assessment as well as a positive value. If measurement errors were temporally correlated, the correction would instead be $2[\sigma_m^2-C_\nu(\ell)]$, where $C_\nu$ is their covariance at lag $\ell$. A constant subtraction would then need revision. <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerLeads = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p64 sha256 b34ca6facaa856c70d89c10707022b3451e9117295904d69214d8b204a9b611d -->
p64, lines 64-64; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p58; next: p66

> For a constructed Brownian movement with $D=0.010$ deg$^2$/s, adding independent position measurement errors with $2\sigma_m^2=0.0010$ deg$^2$ shifts every positive-lag MSD upward by the same amount.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p66 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p66, lines 66-66; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p64; next: p70

> <div class="figure-scroll">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p70 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p70, lines 70-70; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p66; next: p72

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p72 sha256 9f95252d9ebe37997616bdd85ad76c0bf4002c6d41693872d79f7af632e1ece9 -->
p72, lines 72-74; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p70; next: p78

> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO --> The Brownian movement is chosen only to make the constant offset visible. See [Measurement noise correction, Eqs. 18–19, p. 10](../papers/ben-shushan-2022-fixational-drift.pdf#page=10). <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p85 sha256 f6b6c60c5013a8cf2cb9e64d71fc73aafaf166019b7696f53ddb0246da4470d3 -->
p85, lines 85-87; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p78; next: p89

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = NO --> Ordinary diffusion has $M=2D\ell$ and $\alpha=1$. A roughly constant velocity over a short interval gives displacement $v\ell$ and $M=\mathbb E[v^2]\ell^2$, so $\alpha=2$. [Growth faster than linear is called [**superdiffusion**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}. Here it describes a finite range of lags. It does not assert a single power law for arbitrarily long times.]{.definition-explanation}[^p2-msd-result] <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p89 sha256 b8ed3da732bc5f02edda4abea5b2865c4b8164ce64d9399b3ef7c30eb77e032c -->
p89, lines 89-93; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p85; next: p99

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = NO --> Could temporally uncorrelated motor noise acquire the observed MSD just by passing through muscles? [A [**low pass filter**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} suppresses rapid input changes relative to slow ones.]{.definition-explanation} [[**White noise**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} here is an ideal zero mean input whose fluctuations at different times are uncorrelated. In continuous time its strength is defined by an intensity multiplying a delta function, rather than a finite variance at an individual time.]{.definition-explanation} [A stable, decaying response to this noise produces a [**stationary process**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} in the sense needed here: its mean is constant and its covariance depends only on time separation.]{.definition-explanation} Its position variance consequently does not grow with absolute time. Let $E=h*\xi$, where <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p99 sha256 c5ee64d67fb4139bb2976e02abe152ebbb6c401c04baf0b20e4e9df6433c5397 -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p112 sha256 59e3cf4dc2ea0f1acb513d2b0bc24bc18a9397ccfc03a355da9c26349cd6ac49 -->

The MSD consequently approaches $2C_E(0)$, a finite plateau. This reconstructs the reasoning of Eqs. 20–22.[^p2-white-noise] <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

> For the one exponential filter $h(u)=\Theta(u)e^{-u/\tau}/\tau$,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p127 sha256 567a6f8b08ffe904a9cd0be0f622edfb43ed136a86dbdb6dba94c99e7a72861a -->
p127, lines 127-127; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p120; next: p129

> This simple filter has a short lag MSD proportional to $\ell$ and a long lag plateau. A double exponential filter is smoother at short lags, but still gives a plateau because its kernel also decays. Increasing $A$ raises the curve without removing its flattening. The paper's simulated private OMN noise, passed through muscle dynamics, is both too small and too flat at long lags. In Fig. 3b, using 2,000 heterogeneous OMNs, its contribution is about 1% of the observed variance near 900 ms.[^p2-msd-result]

> <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO --> Filtered white noise cannot explain continued long-lag growth. Suppose instead the input is a stored position $B(t)$ undergoing Brownian motion with $\mathbb E[(B(t+\ell)-B(t))^2]=2D\ell$. Its position variance accumulates. A low pass filter with nonzero static gain smooths the path while retaining that accumulating long time displacement variance. The following one exponential example is a teaching reconstruction, not the paper's full muscle model. It derives how smooth movement can have a slope between one and two even though the stored position undergoes ordinary diffusion. <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p142 sha256 e947a3d4f4a1b23611f8aa204b2bb149785cfb02f5ccc687d1eb10b059e0d5aa -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p148 sha256 c2ac4649e3846023d4db09df3f0771ad94abc5706d2f6fbc1dfdb8d4bc1a6360 -->
p148, lines 148-148; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p142; next: p150

> Here $B$ is the changing position command and $E$ is the eye angle that follows it, both in degrees. The mismatch $B-E$ drives velocity: a command to the right of the current position makes the eye move right. For the same mismatch, a larger response time $\tau$ gives a smaller velocity, so the eye takes longer to catch up and smooths rapid command changes more strongly. This response time belongs to the simplified eye filter. It is not automatically the synaptic decay time used in the memory model.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p150 sha256 03177a907301b9e678f307fdd80b75bf6bb748779998697b242e77837f2c9dad -->
p150, lines 150-150; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p148; next: p156

> $W$ is a Brownian process with unit increment variance per unit time, so $D$ has units deg$^2$/s. Define velocity $v=\dot E=(B-E)/\tau$. Taking its increment gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p156 sha256 e6a244842c79df64de991f24286c67e421ac4195c94efdc7ca78d717bf851983 -->
p156, lines 156-156; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p150; next: p158

> Velocity loses its past value over time $\tau$ and receives new random input. To add the surviving effect of those inputs, divide the past into short intervals of width $h$. The Brownian change $\Delta W_j=W(u_j+h)-W(u_j)$ has mean zero and variance $h$. Changes in disjoint intervals are independent. Its contribution to the velocity at time $t$ is approximately $(\sqrt{2D}/\tau)e^{-(t-u_j)/\tau}\Delta W_j$: the initial change has decayed between $u_j$ and $t$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p158 sha256 5d97189c89ebf4c76b56dfefcd0a5a1bb1c630861bca2706cc78866f88ba387e -->
p158, lines 158-160; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p156; next: p166

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = NO --> The integral against $dW(u)$ denotes the limit of these weighted sums as $h$ tends to zero. It does not require an ordinary derivative of the Brownian path. After the initial velocity has decayed, extending the input history into the distant past gives <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p166 sha256 f9ba08be70d412bd4c74a1e31baad98348acedcc85eeeba07f94d0a5188cd4b2 -->
p166, lines 166-166; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p158; next: p178

> For a lag $s\geq0$, multiply the sums for $v(t)$ and $v(t+s)$ and average. Products of distinct increments have zero mean. The same increment contributes its variance $h$, so the sum becomes an ordinary overlap integral over their shared past:

> Holding the internal diffusion coefficient $D$ fixed, increasing $\tau$ reduces short-lag displacement and moves the transition to linear growth to longer lags. It does not stop accumulating error: the long-lag MSD growth rate still approaches $2D$. Increasing $D$ instead raises the entire MSD curve in proportion, at fixed $\tau$. The local logarithmic slope of this MSD is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p210 sha256 795ebe40e736840d0c919b47ef836fbcb60881fbe68838b493d8d88b7e574766 -->
p210, lines 210-212; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p203; next: p214

[Thus an intermediate slope can arise from filtering ordinary diffusion, without faster than Brownian accumulation inside the memory circuit.]{.key-statement} <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

Those ingredients change the detailed curve. The one exponential derivation teaches the distinction relevant to Fig. 3: [a decaying low pass filter of stationary white noise saturates, whereas the same filter, with nonzero static gain, retains accumulating displacement from a wandering position. A slow correlated peripheral source is a different hypothesis from the temporally uncorrelated noise rejected by this comparison.]{.key-statement} <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->

## ch:EYE-CIRCUIT - A noisy memory circuit can produce eye drift

Source file: `content/chapters/04c-eye-circuit.md`

Purpose: A circuit that stores the intended eye angle is one possible source of fixational drift. This chapter follows the proposed model from interacting neurons through motor neurons and muscles to eye motion, and explains how delayed visual feedback can limit that motion.

Prerequisites: ch:EYE-DRIFT, ch:EYE-TIMING, ch:MEMORY-INFORMATION

Sidecar sections: A noisy integrator can reproduce drift after muscle filtering, Spike regularity and circuit parameters set the model's drift scale, The figures test shared drive, diffusion, and the full circuit model, The evidence places shared drive upstream without identifying one circuit

Blocks: 9 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

### A noisy integrator can reproduce drift after muscle filtering

Location: line 9, anchor `sec:EYE-CIRCUIT-MODEL`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 11
Closing passage line: 125

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p11 sha256 0e0e53a5bda11f6c5ef36728ce46544e770033584994c842e69366b663581111 -->
p11, lines 11-11; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p7; next: p13

> The recording analysis places much of the drive upstream of OMNs. To test whether integrator noise could supply it, the authors build a model from a noisy memory circuit through to the eye. The model adapts an earlier goldfish integrator architecture to primate tuning and variability. The model has two groups of integrator neurons. One group fires more when the eye points farther right. The other fires more when it points farther left. Neurons excite cells in their own group and inhibit cells in the other group. The connection strengths are tuned so that the circuit can approximately maintain a range of represented eye positions.[^p2-network]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p13 sha256 2c55ee2c6b6164e165046b3628b1610bb123b45dafe2dc76be02dc1cdcf2a568 -->
p13, lines 13-13; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p11; next: p20

> For the right population, the model's rate is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p20 sha256 670a618c0d9e32cdfcced57c0ccebb39ddc8097dbe087aa1f4cefc79375840e2 -->
p20, lines 20-20; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p13; next: p22

> $E_{OI}$ is the internally represented position in degrees, $\zeta_i^R$ is a tuning slope in spikes/(s deg), and $\lambda_i^0$ is a rate offset in spikes/s. Taking the maximum with zero prevents negative firing. $F$ is a feedback input in degrees, defined below. The matching left neuron has the opposite slope, $\zeta_i^L=-\zeta_i^R$. In an active neuron, a change of one degree changes its rate by $\zeta_i$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p22 sha256 c7ff79e27f16380433894a1124e9b3b4e9a626f712e6e58ea8324738580c53cf -->
p22, lines 22-22; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p20; next: p30

> The model scales each spike train into an input $X_i$ with a saturating mean response. This input changes the neuron's synaptic output $S_i$, the lingering effect of its spikes on neurons receiving them. A weighted difference between the two groups' synaptic outputs gives the represented eye angle $E_{OI}$. This value sets the firing rates again, closing the feedback loop:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p30 sha256 de296cd2e9d8ba9d0fef53592e5154da93708c26aca165aa8f804d895ffbf90d -->
p30, lines 30-32; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p22; next: p34

> <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = NO --> Here $\xi_i^R$ is a spike train generated at rate $\lambda_i^R$, $\tau_s$ is the synaptic decay time, and $\lambda_t=60$ Hz sets the rate scale in the nonlinear synaptic response. Treating spikes as counts, $X_i$ and $S_i$ are dimensionless, and the weights $\eta_i$ are in degrees. Between spikes, $S_i$ decays exponentially. One spike adds a jump of $1/[\tau_s(\lambda_t+\lambda_i^R)]$. Replacing spikes by their mean rate gives a drive $\lambda_i^R/(\lambda_t+\lambda_i^R)$ that approaches one as the rate increases. This is the model's saturating synaptic effect, rather than the purely linear synapses of the basic 2012 example.[^p2-network] <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p34 sha256 f1993f1ed276e39b91f77540c6e110be2e516ea8ea611c4c6121c4592f38473d -->
p34, lines 34-34; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p30; next: p42

> Persistence requires a further condition on that feedback. Set $F=0$ and replace spikes by their mean rates. Define the mean saturated drives at a proposed stored value $z$ by

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p42 sha256 4e85e73974ec9b54a93f3b2d6ada8057939635f18aab8c0a679c6ecf72ab1cf3 -->
p42, lines 42-42; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p34; next: p50

> Multiply each synaptic equation by its readout weight, subtract the left contribution, and sum. Since the same $\tau_s$ applies to every unit, the readout obeys

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p50 sha256 5083d6656a1108ee9b50c50251f393a8c4d0029f07a2ffa02f0f59174a6fec37 -->
p50, lines 50-50; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p42; next: p57

> [An exact continuum of persistent readout values would require $G(z)=z$ over the represented range.]{.key-statement} For a small displacement from a fixed value, Taylor expansion gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p57 sha256 1e97c944f0fc450c1784737f8f8638bc11b02086b712d4aa39edc0c1c80d628f -->
p57, lines 57-59; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p50; next: p61

> <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO --> Thus $G'(z)=1$ is the local neutral condition for the stored coordinate. Stable directions of the remaining synaptic state are also needed for an attractor. The weights were optimized to approximate the fixed-point relation across $-50$ to $50$ degrees. [Residual mismatch can produce deterministic drift, so this finite optimized circuit provides approximate persistence rather than an exactly neutral continuum.]{.key-statement} The tuning slopes and thresholds were sampled to match reported macaque relationships and variation. With spikes, perturbations along the nearly neutral direction also accumulate. This reduction is a teaching derivation from the paper's equations. Figure 4 separates the resulting internal diffusion from later muscle smoothing.[^p2-network] <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p61 sha256 16a7550faf237df16de1714225861cdde1377a31585f50fb80cadb6668e385df -->

p70, lines 70-70; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p67; next: p78

> The motor neurons receive this position signal:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p78 sha256 f3616af329235b1c54c9d26886ea42bd3dc07063311099a99ec70f5a3353d871 -->
p78, lines 78-78; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p70; next: p80

> $\lambda_i^M$ is a motor rate in spikes/s, $E_{T_i}$ is that neuron's position threshold in degrees, and $k_i$ is its position sensitivity. $\xi_i^M$ is its generated spike train. The filtered output $s_i^M$ also has units spikes/s, with decay time $\tau_{MN}$. One spike makes a jump of $1/\tau_{MN}$. At constant mean rate, the mean output is that rate.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p80 sha256 ddcb220d0dfb7202998c334244af8f386a828df6a8544acf98825fc373b6d8b8 -->
p80, lines 80-80; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p78; next: p87

> Each motor unit's muscle and plant response is represented by a position contribution $E_i$ satisfying

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p87 sha256 5f04db9f845b66af5eb517eea90c5318667dc5b8e4135633d6f956852d76ca15 -->
p87, lines 87-93; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p80; next: p95

> <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> These are Eqs. 28–31. The second order equation uses the same roles and units as the forward fit. At constant $E_{OI}$, an active unit has mean $s_i^M=k_i(E_{OI}-E_{T_i})$, giving mean $E_i=E_{OI}$ when derivatives vanish. Averaging therefore transmits the common position while reducing independent private fluctuations. This is an effective model of combined motor output, not a claim that one motor unit moves an isolated eye of its own.[^p2-motor] <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p95 sha256 136a8be33cc7785e4a5baecf0bb1832cea6b8f9f8a858e32423042a32d9d832c -->
p95, lines 95-95; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p87; next: p97

> The model used diverse thresholds and response coefficients, with motor thresholds sampled between $-45$ and $-5$ degrees, and imposed $m_i<r_i^2/(4k_i)$ to give nonoscillatory individual response kernels. Main Methods report $N=30{,}000$ integrator neurons, $N_m=1{,}000$ OMNs, and $\tau_s=20$ ms for the main full simulation. The separate private OMN comparison in Fig. 3 used 2,000 OMNs. These neuron counts belong to different simulation comparisons.[^p2-motor]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p97 sha256 9d128461453cf9975df1673799977098c6bd812722c2fac5a80cd470ac0bffee -->
p97, lines 97-97; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p95; next: p104

> As the eye moves while viewing a stationary target, the target's image moves across the retina. The authors assume that visual feedback responds to this recent image motion rather than to an absolute target-position error. Their delayed feedback model is:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p104 sha256 f1c3e1b8536d884232bf191cae3b0f4b54993b477d5d5b3c76a30b37889dfb1c -->
p104, lines 104-104; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p97; next: p106

> Both positions are in degrees, so $A$ is dimensionless in this formulation. The main value is $A=0.015$. If gaze and its internal representation have recently moved rightward, the delayed position is to their left, making $F$ negative. Its contribution to the integrator rates then opposes continued rightward movement. For a slowly varying position that the eye tracks, $E(t-\tau_d)-E_{OI}(t)\simeq-\tau_d\dot E(t)$, showing the velocity-like action and sign. The approximation requires tracking and slow change over the delay.[^p2-feedback]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p106 sha256 9801dc39d492448ed8de4dbf30c1854a4dd827e29020d55005c98e3139374153 -->
p106, lines 106-108; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p104; next: p110

> <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO --> The effect on the ongoing MSD must be checked from the coupled model, rather than inferred from the delay alone. A short MSD lag is a separation between two samples in a running process, not the time since a new perturbation. Past measurements can already affect both samples, and $F$ also contains the immediate term $-A E_{OI}(t)$. Extended Data Fig. 3 compares the model with and without feedback: feedback changes amplitude across lags, while its larger effect on logarithmic slope occurs at longer lags. Figure 5 shows the full model. [Muscle filtering supplies the short-lag smoothness, and feedback improves the detailed longer-lag fit. These are results of this model comparison, not a hard delay-based cutoff or a claim excluding visual control under other tasks.]{.key-statement}[^p2-feedback] <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04c-eye-circuit.md::sec:EYE-SCALING -->

### Spike regularity and circuit parameters set the model's drift scale

Location: line 128, anchor `sec:EYE-SCALING`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 130
Closing passage line: 198

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p130 sha256 7268e3acf72316b17619c51f8e4affbd93acdc801164f24e37a4ead40e9a2f07 -->
p130, lines 130-130; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p125; next: p132

> An agreement in amplitude is informative only if we know how much freedom the model has. The paper uses regular spike trains. The [spike variability section](ch-foundations.html#sec-foundations-poisson) defined the interspike interval coefficient of variation (CV) as the interval standard deviation divided by its mean. A Poisson process at constant rate has CV one. The model uses about 0.22 for integrator neurons and about 0.07 for OMNs. [Such firing is called [**sub-Poisson spiking**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} here because its long window count variability is lower than a Poisson process at the same rate, under the interval construction used in the model.]{.definition-explanation}[^p2-cv]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p132 sha256 ee2f5e50f8c9e03685d56355242bbd0db05bdcb1b862f1d9ef36e048ddf3ed97 -->
p132, lines 132-134; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p130; next: p136

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> The construction starts with a Poisson process at $M$ times the desired rate and retains every $M$th spike. At constant rate, a retained interval is the sum of $M$ independent exponential intervals. Each initial interval has mean $1/(M\lambda)$ and variance $1/(M\lambda)^2$. The retained interval therefore has mean $1/\lambda$, variance $1/(M\lambda^2)$, and CV $1/\sqrt M$. This preserves the mean rate while reducing variability. Its use at changing rates is part of the model. The simple interval derivation assumes a constant rate. <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p136 sha256 f2a7fb2203b6e498df53e64bc114989a1beaa99bf6efc794ebd412e682ffa4c7 -->
p136, lines 136-138; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p132; next: p146

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> At constant rate, the retained intervals are independent. [This is a [**renewal process**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, a spike process whose successive intervals are independent and identically distributed.]{.definition-explanation} Let their mean be $\mu=1/\lambda$ and standard deviation $\sigma_I$. The time to $n$ spikes has variance $n\sigma_I^2$. For a long fixed window $L\simeq n\mu$, a fluctuation in total interval time produces the opposite fluctuation in count divided by $\mu$. Thus <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p146 sha256 2bc134b802d4a537f31e94ff71be2e235dfbb232bd3bb07565c61e78c0d34fbf -->
p146, lines 146-146; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p136; next: p148

> This explains why the diffusion scale contains CV squared: it is the strength of the accumulated spike fluctuations relative to the Poisson case. The [diffusion calculation](ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-diffusion) and [information bound](ch-memory-information.html#sec-memory-bound) show how projecting those fluctuations onto a neutral memory direction adds a factor proportional to $1/\tau_s^2$ and, for a comparable independent population, $1/N$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p148 sha256 ea92a5944f6936bf14c08b8eb5a686309ccd3a64074dcb0fbd8b0fd3d02a5a2f -->
p148, lines 148-148; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p146; next: p154

> Rate and sensitivity need more care. Write the absolute local rate slope as $a=d\lambda/dE$, in spikes/(s deg). If $N$ similar cells fire near rate $\lambda_0$, their Poisson information per unit time about position is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p154 sha256 361a2c878568912eb901ca5bab4ef2f7d63e7d0202cf670dc8960c97eae9eaf1 -->
p154, lines 154-154; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p148; next: p161

> Using the [information-diffusion relation](ch-memory-information.html#sec-memory-bound) as a scale, including the $\mathrm{CV}^2$ factor just derived and holding the network geometry fixed,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p161 sha256 a97e949c6ae8180c1ec415fd1e133720415886dedd04e3fdde70ef41afb02e93 -->
p161, lines 161-161; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p154; next: p167

> If instead sensitivity is expressed as the fractional slope $b=a/\lambda_0$, in inverse degrees, the same expression becomes

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p167 sha256 e192468e25be0c362086e4d9b7b4a08106cb75f343ef2d93f1fb38c3b0139324 -->
p167, lines 167-171; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p161; next: p173

> This second form has the dependence printed in the paper's Eq. 1, $D\propto\mathrm{CV}^2N^{-1}\lambda_0^{-1}k_0^{-2}\tau_s^{-2}$. <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO --> However, its surrounding text calls $k_0$ position sensitivity without specifying fractional normalization. If $k_0$ means an absolute slope in Hz/degree, that printed expression has the wrong units for $D$. The version with $\lambda_0$ in the numerator has the required units deg$^2$/s. This scaling is a comparison under fixed network geometry and noise assumptions, not an exact formula for every recurrent circuit.[^p2-scaling] <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p173 sha256 42108d5c0778252cbcd35d52f426aa9e76c329e923fd7b375471446ca7dd953a -->
p173, lines 173-173; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p167; next: p175

> At fixed network geometry and under the noise assumptions above, the scale changes as follows:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p175 sha256 34adbe757bd5affe2fb5b060f436bc1fca3237c2ff8374e071eb38fe8056c78b -->
p175, lines 175-175; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p173; next: p176

> Doubling $N$ halves it.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p176 sha256 97eca66015ea66b16c7b4fcd50e9b39053a11b090620a6593b38a378a7f8817d -->
p176, lines 176-176; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p175; next: p177

> Doubling CV multiplies it by four.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p177 sha256 7041a8942a6d60632e76aa235eebc13fc76689b7c5b5a105d7defaf4d65f0af1 -->
p177, lines 177-177; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p176; next: p178

> Doubling $\tau_s$ divides it by four.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p178 sha256 c69f6d406939a6610f247f8d90681c726715052db1426b91bbea5c0677918062 -->
p178, lines 178-178; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p177; next: p180

> Doubling the absolute slope at fixed rate also divides it by four.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p180 sha256 d580379a231f1928d507b8cc9ffee1e1a1ce2897c439f84943770e16eeced78e -->
p180, lines 180-180; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p178; next: p182

> [Increasing rate reduces diffusion when the fractional slope is fixed, because more spikes then also supply a proportionally steeper absolute slope. Increasing rate at fixed absolute slope is a different comparison and increases the scale in the first formula. A rate change alone does not specify which comparison is intended.]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p182 sha256 96cd80b8d962767580705fe9c3c707439e072e0abeb2940437a471e7a6524b04 -->
p182, lines 182-182; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p180; next: p189

> Could the measured CV itself mostly reflect drift rather than private spike variability? Supplementary Notes test this. Let $\lambda(t)=\bar\lambda+aB(t)$ during a fixation of length $T$, with the rate changes small relative to $\bar\lambda$. For a Brownian path starting at zero, the expected time averaged squared position is $DT$, and the expected squared time mean is $2DT/3$. Subtracting gives the expected within-segment position variance $DT/3=2DT/6$:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p189 sha256 72378fdd398d6e247d1a7cc8ca3b5e733fe43ff9b996e8ca59dadc25f329176a -->
p189, lines 189-191; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p182; next: p198

> The second term follows from $\mathbb E[B(t)B(s)]=2D\min(t,s)$ and $T^{-2}\int_0^T\int_0^T2D\min(t,s)\,dt\,ds=2DT/3$. Expanding $1/(\bar\lambda+aB)$ to first order gives $1/\bar\lambda-aB/\bar\lambda^2$. Dividing its standard deviation by its mean then gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p198 sha256 daec62b72416c9fe5cbf7b7a661d9e0b35afa56ea2d887bb1b9ea3c1b32c333b -->
p198, lines 198-200; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p189; next: p204

> <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO --> With the supplement's values $a=1/15$ spikes/(s arcmin), $2D=100$ arcmin$^2$/s, $T=0.5$ s, and $\bar\lambda=80$ Hz, this is about $0.0024$, well below the OMN CV near 0.07. These are an order of magnitude check, not a fitted diffusion constant for the whole model. Since one degree is 60 arcmin, that $2D$ equals $0.0278$ deg$^2$/s, or $D=0.0139$ deg$^2$/s.[^p2-cv-drift] <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->

> The most useful supplemental comparisons are Extended Data Fig. 1 for individual uncertainty, Fig. 2 for noise subtraction and its simulated control, Fig. 3 for feedback, Fig. 4 for a no-central-drive control, and Fig. 5 for the other monkey's fit. In the no-central-drive control, $\widehat\chi=-0.09\pm0.22$ is compatible with zero. That negative estimate is sampling variation. Figures 6–8 document cell classification, segment sampling, and a rank correlation check. Figure 9 shows the heterogeneous model parameters. Figure 10 separates spiking noise passed directly to the output from spiking noise that drives diffusion inside the integrator: internal stochastic spikes remain in the latter comparison. Its caption reports little direct output-noise effect beyond 50 ms.[^p2-supp-figures]

### The evidence places shared drive upstream without identifying one circuit

Location: line 270, anchor `sec:EYE-LIMITS`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 272
Closing passage line: 321

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p272 sha256 3727eeedee8c692577a6aa4b7d7d89df3be4c829c3bbeba33d2513e64747986b -->
p272, lines 272-272; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p267; next: p274

> The data and model answer different questions about where the drive originates:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p274 sha256 13eb9938bf9c6236208fc52e01c9ec1269b4cf02460ab63085476a39b268d851 -->
p274, lines 274-274; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p272; next: p275

> The common variance estimate supports a drive upstream of OMNs, given the calibration and noise assumptions.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p275 sha256 0e5d9875c42e7d1eba8d510a5e8714c4a462cd405dbbf43cc17a69bdb9d4f8c6 -->
p275, lines 275-275; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p274; next: p276

> The MSD comparison supports a command that accumulates variation over the measured interval, rather than stationary white noise passed through a decaying muscle filter.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p276 sha256 a53b626e8087382134210e2ebd356cd81cc400d4dac48406313cec70350ad76b -->
p276, lines 276-276; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p275; next: p278

> The circuit model shows that integrator noise with plausible parameters can generate such a command and explain its filtered amplitude and timing.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p278 sha256 c425044283e40e9b6f778b278f78adba97712f7ed301f9f298a85114fd6f8a34 -->
p278, lines 278-280; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p276; next: p282

> <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO --> Those results do not uniquely locate the noise inside integrator neurons. Two missing tests limit that conclusion: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p282 sha256 169e07537880886865c6648a569b2f2faed5ef6763e39c675090f1cb6167a2ea -->
p282, lines 282-282; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p278; next: p283

> <!-- vale ai-tells.NegatedSubject = NO -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p283 sha256 ab759ba506da07a020a7d3c5660dcd20dd15d60d477ca4d9eba2af59d4889882 -->
p283, lines 283-284; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p282; next: p285

> No integrator neurons were recorded in this study. <!-- vale ai-tells.NegatedSubject = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p285 sha256 617ad29267837c3232f4196d91fd6ba06c2ea3c2d71dbb9ea776f3ddffac9cc6 -->
p285, lines 285-285; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p283; next: p287

> No selective change of that circuit tested whether it was necessary for drift.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p287 sha256 ba65fa56bc0d3faafbfc995dc46aba3604de7dcd48e6d201336e7ff0644e2fd9 -->
p287, lines 287-287; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p285; next: p289

> [Noisy inputs to the integrator, or an already diffusive upstream signal, remain possible. The authors make this distinction in the Discussion and explicitly in their peer review response. Several combinations of neuron count, variability, and synaptic time constant can also fit an amplitude, so matching one curve does not uniquely recover those parameters.]{.key-statement}[^p2-localization]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p289 sha256 f30a3f26207223383b4a4d06da014792ed71a792843af70658a8a0ee8cce6273 -->
p289, lines 289-291; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p287; next: p293

> <!-- vale ai-tells.ServesAsDodge = NO --> The study's empirical scope is horizontal motion during these fixation segments in two animals. Visual tasks, feedback, and other sources of input can alter the statistics. Its contribution to the connection with the 2012 theory is more precise: a continuous memory can diffuse internally, while a mechanical output of that memory looks smooth and superdiffusive at shorter lags. To compare a memory theory with behavior, the mapping from stored value to measured movement is part of the calculation. <!-- vale ai-tells.ServesAsDodge = YES -->

## ch:PLACE-FIELDS - Field counts and gaps reveal threshold and scale

Source file: `content/chapters/05-place-fields.md`

Purpose: Hippocampal place cells fire more at particular locations as an animal moves. This chapter introduces the rodent and bat recordings, then shows how a smooth random input that exceeds a firing threshold can produce several separate active regions for one cell.

Prerequisites: ch:FOUNDATIONS, ch:PERSISTENCE

Sidecar sections: Thresholding a smooth Gaussian input produces place fields, Upcrossings count fields, with an endpoint correction, Mean field and gap lengths separate threshold from scale

Blocks: 3 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected
