<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p3 sha256 29d0403b6f10ebc3606109ccc4c5106c259d763f0c2cf0fd8a3800b9c7415043 -->
p3, lines 3-5; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: none; next: p7

> <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO --> The study compares recordings of hippocampal place cells from bats flying through tunnels, rats moving over an arena floor, and bats flying through a room. For tunnel flights, the analysis uses distance along an approximately one-dimensional route, although the tunnel itself has width and height. The floor map uses two position coordinates, and the room map uses three. <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p7 sha256 6690f9b94e6e8ae6d89fe5007cc730d3276602809cbb5c4f1b71272581befb9f -->
p7, lines 7-7; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p3; next: p9

> A cell recorded on a short track may have one smooth firing hill. [On a long track, the same kind of cell may have several separated fields, each with a different width and height. In a room, its active regions can bend, split, and surround inactive holes. How could one model produce this variety and still make useful predictions?]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p9 sha256 1cbd41f3b1495115a9573dd5e2510141728376a0b6244ff27745ba64d63e1582 -->
p9, lines 9-13; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p7; next: p15

> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = NO --> Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak propose that a cell's spatial input is a smooth random function. This function can be interpreted as the average combined input arriving through the cell's synapses at each animal position. The model cell has positive firing rate where this input exceeds a threshold. The recordings estimate firing rates rather than measuring this input directly.[^p3-model] [The model predicts distributions of field lengths, areas, and volumes, rather than the location of a particular cell's next field. Its strongest tests check whether fitting field sizes and counts also explains field shapes and holes.]{.key-statement}[^p3-question] <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p15 sha256 e1e6980f6d60db858b1d25b5719fef33a26036773dc5a7b975a816b801d9852b -->
p15, lines 15-15; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p9; next: p17

> We begin on a track, where a field starts at [an [**upcrossing**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, a point where the input crosses upward through the threshold.]{.definition-explanation} Counting these crossings gives the average number of fields. Combining that count with the lengths of active fields and inactive gaps then separates the effect of the threshold from the distance over which the map changes. These calculations establish the parameters needed for the later predictions of sizes and shapes.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p17 sha256 8fdab90b4b34de6de1bcb6d39e9aae308c919ff7378e7b6d6cf2b4cbe993a21c -->
p17, lines 17-17; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p15; next: p21

> [Spatial randomness here describes different possible rate maps. Once a map is drawn, it is fixed while the animal moves through it. The spatial coordinate is not time, and the model does not by itself describe memory drift, spike timing, or learning.]{.key-statement}

### Thresholding a smooth Gaussian input produces place fields

Location: line 19, anchor `sec:PLACE-GP`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 21
Closing passage line: 117

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p21 sha256 cf3931630bd66d71de8518d443fc4b71f41ade0a3e68c41d522d4c3108417994 -->
p21, lines 21-21; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p17; next: p23

> To predict a field's width, we need to relate activity at nearby positions. A histogram of firing rates alone loses that information: rearranging its values could turn one broad field into many tiny fields without changing the histogram.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p23 sha256 4f57b493be9c7b2282e101a14133a60a100c9e30bd8a1acf9a0f314308311f3c -->
p23, lines 23-23; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p21; next: p27

> The model assigns an input $h(x)$ to each position $x$, with nearby values linked so that a cell can have a continuous active region. [A [**Gaussian process**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} specifies a distribution of such maps.]{.definition-explanation} Its mean sets the average input, and its spatial covariance describes how inputs at two positions vary together across possible maps. The chosen covariance must also make the maps smooth enough for the derivatives used below. After a map is drawn, moving the animal changes which position is evaluated, not the map itself.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p27 sha256 795571a9c7522b8f232015c6826830d7be9965b8534c71ddf7babec80ebc8c16 -->
p27, lines 27-29; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p23; next: p36

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> [A Gaussian process is a probability distribution over functions such that the values at any finite set of positions form a jointly Gaussian random vector.]{.definition-explanation} For positions $x_1,\ldots,x_n$, the vector $(h(x_1),\ldots,h(x_n))$ therefore has a multivariate Gaussian distribution. Its mean vector and covariance matrix are obtained by evaluating two functions, <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p36 sha256 66c88dd3c318a02c44cbf80b7d5975c5e7a4f86028a74f27305662cd00c92b0e -->
p36, lines 36-38; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p27; next: p42

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> The expectation runs over possible maps, or over cells assumed to share these statistics. These two functions determine every finite joint distribution. A Gaussian process can thus produce many different curves while keeping a precise rule for their spatial dependence. [A [**jointly Gaussian vector**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is one for which every linear combination of its entries is Gaussian.]{.definition-explanation} For this family, zero covariance between two subvectors implies their independence. That extra property, which does not hold for general distributions, will simplify the crossing calculation. The values after thresholding will generally not have a Gaussian distribution.[^p3-model] <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p42 sha256 74bba45fced84d1ad39044216a1943a68293c7e36e21eec6f996c69cd418f9c0 -->
p42, lines 42-44; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p36; next: p46

> <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO --> Three assumptions have different jobs: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p46 sha256 ff020e747f93c7758851b1dd37fc6eacad9096410af0ebc2611c15bba44457aa -->
p46, lines 46-46; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p42; next: p47

> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = NO -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p47 sha256 fa5ef2482e5bc1daf167533dbeba79af7e4c94508b2fbb2b45873a9c75e68383 -->
p47, lines 47-49; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p46; next: p50

> [[**Stationarity**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} means that moving the observation window does not change the map's probability law. Here it gives a constant mean and a covariance $C(x,y)=C(y-x)$ that depends on separation.]{.definition-explanation} It does not say that a particular map is flat. <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p50 sha256 601746d58efe3ed53961451d37695488ab02dd38d52a01c2bd98b0f891e434e3 -->
p50, lines 50-51; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p47; next: p52

> [[**Isotropy**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} means that rotating the separation does not change the covariance: in several dimensions, $C(\Delta x)$ depends only on $\|\Delta x\|$.]{.definition-explanation} It rules out a preferred direction in the model's statistics. Stationarity alone does not imply isotropy. <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p52 sha256 918c7f153e6c52c9c3c244626b4ce88ba16b6ffceccb4d0deaa7b7684cc627a5 -->
p52, lines 52-52; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p50; next: p54

> [[**Smoothness**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} controls whether the map has well defined spatial derivatives.]{.definition-explanation} Crossing calculations require differentiable sample functions and nondegenerate joint distributions: the relevant vector of heights and derivatives must have an invertible covariance matrix, rather than obey an exact linear constraint. Peak calculations require second derivatives and enough regularity to control their local approximation.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p54 sha256 42eb0e3e0dcd3af5f2ba6ca79b946c07be98ec89d485906ead6ce711916a8d27 -->
p54, lines 54-56; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p52; next: p58

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> A Gaussian distribution of values does not guarantee this smoothness. For example, a covariance proportional to $e^{-|x-y|/b}$ has a sharp corner at zero separation and does not supply the differentiable paths assumed below. <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p58 sha256 f19aba453f15007b3477fd225c1ceac7a62a4b58c1fa0af170d4819308371576 -->
p58, lines 58-58; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p54; next: p66

> Set the mean to zero and write $\sigma_h^2=C(0)$ for input variance. A constant input mean can be absorbed into the threshold. It is useful to normalize the input,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p66 sha256 4eec9e99c3ebedb0dccb9f908af3451aa3647fc0077c3266b76930381b564ac2 -->
p66, lines 66-66; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p58; next: p68

> Here $z$ has variance one, $q$ is a dimensionless threshold, and $a>0$ converts normalized input to firing rate, in Hz. The paper writes $\theta$ for $q$, $r$ for covariance, and $D$ for spatial dimension. This guide uses $q,C,d$ to avoid conflicts with earlier chapters. The original input threshold is $q\sigma_h$. In the paper's Equation 3 the firing rate is proportional to $[h-q\sigma_h]_+$, which is equivalent to this form after choosing the gain.[^p3-model]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p68 sha256 c2c0a9ae4b0c6876561488a46a1819103e947613233287b5cdb1eb3b8335f746 -->
p68, lines 68-68; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p66; next: p70

> The input threshold summarizes how much combined input is needed before the model cell fires. It can reflect the cell's own response properties and inhibitory input. Raising $q$ at a fixed input map leaves fewer positions active. If the absolute threshold above the input mean is positive and held fixed, increasing the input standard deviation $\sigma_h$ lowers $q$, making activity more widespread. Changing $a$ instead changes rates within active regions while preserving their zero-rate boundaries. A fixed positive detection cutoff in Hz would change this last conclusion. [A fitted threshold describes the balance of input and response. It does not identify which biological quantity changed.]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p70 sha256 86a4397dc4e6ababff47289f83f408a10560f133d0d3e5143cceae036c94dbd8 -->
p70, lines 70-72; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p68; next: p76

> [The [**excursion set**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} above $q$ is]{.definition-explanation}

p85, lines 85-89; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p79; next: p91

> <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> Each connected piece of $A_q$ is a model place field. In one dimension its size is length. In two and three dimensions it is area and volume. [Two nearby peaks belong to one field if an above-threshold path joins them. Counting peaks and counting fields therefore answer different questions.]{.key-statement} <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p91 sha256 13a7443113bd7235868647853097382186a2f42f844defb231ad1430a9d7d59a -->

> For a one dimensional stationary process, differentiation of covariance gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p98 sha256 2442f626a333929c7774a980d8033033c9da5bffa9887ab195db0428a595466a -->
p98, lines 98-98; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p91; next: p105

> The minus sign comes from differentiating $y-x$ once with respect to each variable. The paper defines the spatial scale

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p105 sha256 19fb123c8262a417395e57182b49f69727e10d237633b6251599208116129590 -->
p105, lines 105-107; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p98; next: p109

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> Thus $\sigma$ compares typical height with typical slope and has units of distance. In an isotropic field, the same definition applies along any unit direction. For the smooth Gaussian covariance $C(\Delta x)=\sigma_h^2\exp[-\|\Delta x\|^2/(2\sigma^2)]$, it is exactly the parameter in that exponent. The paper uses a Gaussian covariance for its simulations. Other smooth covariances can share this local scale while having different longer distance structure.[^p3-scale] <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p109 sha256 ebdd8b0016bad74b9e16ae42e4d433c8876d5071b8d33a62976ee07fd385b961 -->
p109, lines 109-109; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p105; next: p111

> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO --> Thresholding changes the distribution of values and can separate one smooth input curve into several fields. Both panels describe the same fixed map over space, so neither curve is a trajectory over time. <!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05-place-fields.md::sec:PLACE-KAC-RICE -->

### Upcrossings count fields, with an endpoint correction

Location: line 121, anchor `sec:PLACE-KAC-RICE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 123
Closing passage line: 185

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p123 sha256 217ea3e4f2da80ac8833b311fc1e2bdd3e2bfb97716ddd5f8b3d719e98a6d21b -->
p123, lines 123-123; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p117; next: p125

> A field starts at an upcrossing, so its expected count can be found by counting upward passages through the threshold. A small interval around each crossing has a width determined by its slope. This lets us count crossings without locating every root.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p125 sha256 89750673492344d837763a92c89b3cd3f64839f3a984d2cf64b05477dd7eb0ef -->
p125, lines 125-125; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p123; next: p131

> Consider a differentiable fixed curve and a simple upward crossing $x_0$ of $q$, with slope $v=z'(x_0)>0$. Close to the crossing,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p131 sha256 6089b883f235b74f0e246976ec9a53d25c48043bb68041a337da548749e81a9c -->
p131, lines 131-133; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p125; next: p139

> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO --> The curve lies in the vertical band $|z-q|<\varepsilon$ for a spatial distance approximately $2\varepsilon/v$. Integrating $z'(x)/(2\varepsilon)$ over that interval contributes one. Restricting to positive slopes counts only upward crossings. [Under the usual regularity conditions, averaging this counting identity gives the [**Kac–Rice formula**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} for the mean upcrossing density,]{.definition-explanation} <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p139 sha256 b2ca6c87c97a8ddc19b2da7ea251d7f81efcce17ed2d440c38075a5b5330f8ca -->
p139, lines 139-139; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p131; next: p141

> The density $p_{z,z'}(q,v)$ describes how often height is near $q$ and slope near $v$. Multiplication by $v$ compensates for the shorter spatial window of a steep crossing. Multiplying the probability of being above threshold by a typical slope would miss this weighting.[^p3-crossings]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p141 sha256 30afaa7d71d1c5c40139ed8744ebc9512d14702cffbd326bfdd18012244a2666 -->
p141, lines 141-141; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p139; next: p143

> A fixed smooth curve shows how to count a field already active at the left edge and why the crossing integral weights each slope.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p143 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p143, lines 143-143; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p141; next: p147

> <div class="figure-scroll">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p147 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p147, lines 147-147; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p143; next: p149

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p149 sha256 04bdc367a22c8716b8aab8fa75de3b51d243dc6ec03720e6789b1433a55ff7e3 -->
p149, lines 149-149; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p147; next: p151

> The count includes every connected active interval intersecting the track. Discarding clipped boundary fields would change this convention.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p151 sha256 8c29e2b994c75073980c3f8ad32714b819c26ccc7b35bd53d2de20a9b6433dc4 -->
p151, lines 151-151; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p149; next: p157

> We can evaluate the integral because height and slope are independent here. Stationarity gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p157 sha256 b60df8d1d24656b5a36ecc728e67fb617e727ba58a315ffe20f273281622e8cb -->
p157, lines 157-159; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p151; next: p173

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> Height and slope are jointly Gaussian. Zero covariance therefore makes them independent. Their distributions are $z\sim\mathcal N(0,1)$ and $z'\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma^{-2})$. Consequently, <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p173 sha256 86caccf9cbc38b6a2f07eb40c432b4e57c5951fc2c36b98d104242d36e82e453 -->
p173, lines 173-175; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p157; next: p177

> <!-- vale ai-tells.LabelAndExplain = NO --> In the integral, use $u=\sigma^2v^2/2$, so $v\,dv=du/\sigma^2$. [This result is exact for the stated smooth stationary Gaussian model. A high threshold is not needed.]{.key-statement} Its units are inverse distance: increasing $\sigma$ stretches the map and reduces crossings per metre. <!-- vale ai-tells.LabelAndExplain = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p177 sha256 a3178d05daf5a8383331b6a0a5b1c5e79a3b9e27e140138effb0735de2e92ce6 -->
p177, lines 177-177; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p173; next: p185

> On a finite track $[0,L]$, each upcrossing starts a field, but a field may already be active at the left endpoint. If $N_L$ counts all components intersecting the track, then, under regularity conditions that exclude tangencies and give nonzero slopes at crossings,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p185 sha256 7736661f9fb1708487ba89d75f45e334b953e0f0ba02c2c2a3f68d5fa0f24dad -->
p185, lines 185-185; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p177; next: p189

> where $Q(q)=1-\Phi(q)$ and $\Phi$ is the standard normal cumulative distribution function. The last term is the endpoint correction. Removing incomplete boundary fields changes the counting convention, so the same correction cannot then be used unchanged. This identity also gives the paper's exact one dimensional mean Euler characteristic, Equation 8.[^p3-ec-source]

### Mean field and gap lengths separate threshold from scale

Location: line 187, anchor `sec:PLACE-INFERENCE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 189
Closing passage line: 280

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p189 sha256 6c14fa70c37f2886286fdd9b503c382a38ef32e4e25437d77db2fa616279e50d -->
p189, lines 189-189; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p185; next: p191

> [A low field count can result from a high threshold or a slowly varying map. Field lengths help separate those possibilities.]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p191 sha256 3f685458c1f5bc83a32a1a4770b151648138aa7dacbf06994110817727da7e04 -->
p191, lines 191-193; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p189; next: p204

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> At a uniformly chosen position, the probability of activity is $Q(q)$. Hence a track of length $L$ has expected occupied length $LQ(q)$. On a long stationary track, the average occupied length per field is the occupied length per unit distance divided by the number of fields per unit distance. The corresponding gap calculation uses the inactive fraction $\Phi(q)$: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p204 sha256 6fc750fb0ae48d8804aa2e0a57ba3d7dfaf42a38e0f59cb9564ec1a8f7ab25fd -->
p204, lines 204-204; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p191; next: p206

> These are stationary per-field and per-gap means, ignoring clipping at track boundaries. They are not an assertion that the expectation of a finite sample's ratio equals the ratio of its expectations. Subject to suitable averaging over a long track or many maps, they give the inference rule in Methods S3, Equations S14–S15.[^p3-means]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p206 sha256 302cfe3ed35e922f0fd2c191f88efb35fc98f0654a85332d71b8cd317ac46e33 -->
p206, lines 206-206; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p204; next: p213

> Adding and dividing removes one unknown at a time:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p213 sha256 ca2a8eb7cda241d122db3209e4e9a118a894ce889e6383f964abf54bcfb4ae22 -->
p213, lines 213-215; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p206; next: p223

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> Thus <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p223 sha256 c2ae0c5b7d34227adc8ede41a649d1b00f4db6699eb8d68ebae0f3155328267c -->
p223, lines 223-227; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p213; next: p229

> <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> For example, $q=1.1$ and $\sigma=2.4$ m, the fitted parameters reported for the 200 m bat tunnel, give $Q(q)=0.1357$, $\nu_+=0.03621$ per metre, $\mu_s=3.746$ m, and $\mu_g=23.868$ m in the continuous model. Putting these two means into the inversion recovers $q=1.1$ and $\sigma=2.4$ m. The expected number of intersecting fields on a 200 m track is $7.2425+0.1357=7.3782$, including the left endpoint term. These are calculations from the fitted model, not fresh estimates from raw recordings. <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p229 sha256 36c7fa19663c9c07dd5fa1b3fbc3f04febe71d347428fbc6169a398d4322fbf6 -->
p229, lines 229-235; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p223; next: p237

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO --> [The means do not imply a full gap or count distribution. At high thresholds, distant rare excursions can approach independent events if correlations decay sufficiently with distance. In that regime, field locations approach a Poisson process, counts in a long interval are approximately Poisson, and gaps are approximately exponential. Smoothness and stationarity alone do not ensure this independence.]{.key-statement} A periodic Gaussian process, for example, retains long distance dependence. Upward and downward crossings that enclose the same small field remain strongly related even when fields far apart are almost independent. <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p237 sha256 d8329140074a8cfa78d595d1fb497bf52c7bb51a0b4851af51cb62d749a92fe0 -->
p237, lines 237-237; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p229; next: p246

> Experimental selection also matters. If cells with no fields are excluded, a Poisson model with unconditioned mean $\lambda$ gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p246 sha256 3889a03387b2f0ea6253d52ac9eddfcfc693e052e1672ed237823cf0423ca7d5 -->
p246, lines 246-246; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p237; next: p248

> The mean follows because the omitted $n=0$ term contributes zero to $\sum n\Pr(N=n)=\lambda$, while conditioning divides all remaining probabilities by $1-e^{-\lambda}$. For $\lambda=1$, $36.8\%$ of cells are excluded and the observed mean is $1.582$, not one. Using $1.582$ as the unconditioned mean would infer too many fields. The paper applies this correction to its two dimensional count fit, Equation 5.[^p3-selection]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p248 sha256 72fb9debbff6b02f5de0f17c603a3aa9b077a0b677b3227f9256736ccfda428a -->

## ch:PLACE-SIZES - High peaks explain size laws across dimensions

Source file: `content/chapters/05b-place-sizes.md`

Purpose: A place cell’s active regions can have different sizes on a route, across a floor, or in a flight volume. This chapter derives why the spatial model predicts different field-size distributions in one, two, and three dimensions, and identifies the high-threshold approximation used in that derivation.

Prerequisites: ch:PLACE-FIELDS

Sidecar sections: Near a high peak, the input is nearly parabolic, Geometry gives different size laws in each dimension, Slices test spatial structure beyond field volume, Why slopes at field boundaries are Rayleigh

Blocks: 6 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

p3, lines 3-7; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: none; next: p9

> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO --> [Knowing the average field length does not tell us how often fields fall within a given size range. Why should the same spatial model give one size distribution on a track, another in an arena, and a third in a room?]{.key-statement} The next step is to find the shape of a typical field when only unusually high input peaks rise above the threshold. <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p9 sha256 c29c015dbbfdbad55dc38a150f923ec96052c788bcc2c40e056de70e02bfdce5 -->
p9, lines 9-9; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p3; next: p11

> Near such a peak, the smooth input is approximately a downward parabola. Its height above the threshold sets a radius, and converting that radius into length, area or volume gives the different size laws. Cuts through a map test its spatial structure, and slopes at field edges provide a separate check on the predicted field widths. [The size laws use a high threshold approximation. The boundary-slope law has a separate exact derivation under the smooth stationary Gaussian assumptions.]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p11 sha256 26a1fe7914be17a1e3c5a4d6f987ea769b07b29a63e572b3b3b67ef972df4555 -->
p11, lines 11-11; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p9; next: p15

> We keep the earlier notation: $z(x)$ is the spatial input normalized to variance one, $q$ is its threshold, $\sigma$ is its local distance scale, and $a$ converts positive excess input into firing rate. The number of spatial dimensions is $d$. The firing rule remains $F(x)=a[z(x)-q]_+$, where $[u]_+=\max(u,0)$.

> [The [**Hessian**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is the matrix $H$ of second spatial derivatives. Its eigenvalues describe curvature along the principal directions.]{.definition-explanation} Isotropy and the definition of $\sigma$ imply

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> Here $\delta_{ij}$ is one for equal indices and zero otherwise. For jointly Gaussian variables, conditioning $Y$ on a scalar $Z$ of variance one gives $\mathbb E[Y\mid Z=b]=\operatorname{Cov}(Y,Z)b$ when both means vanish. One way to see this is to write $Y=\operatorname{Cov}(Y,Z)Z+\epsilon$. The remainder has zero covariance with $Z$ and is therefore independent of it. Applying this to each Hessian entry gives <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p43 sha256 47d4b1cf84b128232a6a68f53843a3ec5ff5371866328fc9cd3b8a9d22498674 -->
p43, lines 43-43; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p35; next: p45

> The conditional fluctuations around this mean have a variance that does not grow with $b$. For a high peak, the negative mean curvature is of order $q$, so its relative fluctuations become small. Conditioning the gradient to zero does not change this Gaussian conditional mean, because the gradient has zero covariance with both height and Hessian in a stationary isotropic field. Selecting an actual maximum also weights curvatures, but its leading high peak shape is the same.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p45 sha256 0041a3d93b0436aee52ecdbbc68f4786f15bd183b547921f61c99d063d378053 -->

In one dimension, the number of high maxima is asymptotically the number of upcrossings, proportional to $e^{-q^2/2}$. Therefore the fraction of peaks above $q$ that also exceed $q+\epsilon$ is approximately <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> [Thus the scaled excess height approaches an exponential variable of mean one.]{.key-statement} In several dimensions, high peak counts have the additional leading factor $q^{d-1}$. Its ratio at $q+u/q$ and $q$ tends to one, so the same excess-height limit remains. The higher dimensional count factor will also follow from the [Euler characteristic calculation](ch-place-tests.html#sec-place-topology). A rigorous conclusion requires control of the remainder and of components containing multiple peaks, beyond this local argument. <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = YES -->

> The exponent two concerns rate versus linear size. In three dimensions a suitable linear size is $s^{1/3}$ when $s$ denotes volume.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p96 sha256 e692215e6c67ed6c9451305a30abf1f503c3b4e04040f42ac5a63a561354020b -->
p96, lines 96-100; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p94; next: p102

> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.NounString = NO --> At moderate thresholds the omitted shape fluctuations matter. Figure 3C reports a rate versus length exponent of $1.42$ in the 200 m bat data and $1.46$ in finite threshold model simulations. Figure 3D reports $1.75$ versus $1.71$ using cube root of volume in 3D. These are compatible with a model that approaches exponent two only as the threshold becomes large. They are not measurements of the limiting exponent. Figure 5A–C illustrates the progression toward simpler, usually single peak fields as $q$ increases. Figure 4C tests this trend in the shorter bat tunnel.[^p3-shapes] <!-- vale ai-tells.NounString = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

Write the strongest correction a domain expert would make to this unit's text or figures; set kind to correctness or wording; name its target passage or figure label; set disposition to fixed or does not apply; give the reason. An objection of none is not a review. The strongest correction is the one that would change what a reader believes or does. Aim first at what the unit claims: a promise word such as always, once, ensures or remains that is false or needs a condition; a named method described wrongly; code that does not do what the text says; a table cell; a diagnostic sequence that skips a stage; a verb without an actor; a label naming something other than what is drawn; a figure that disagrees with the text or another figure; and example content found in neither the chapter nor its sources. For each code listing, write test: followed by the path, relative to the guide, of a test that runs it on the inputs the text makes claims about, or not run: followed by the reason and how its claims were checked instead.
Essential explanation cannot be marked Not applicable. Trace the reader's task and its dependencies, including gaps not covered by other fields. Record unresolved: none only after resolving essential gaps; otherwise describe them and keep the unit verdict open. A complete checklist is not proof of complete teaching.

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05b-place-sizes.md::sec:PLACE-SIZE-LAW -->

### Geometry gives different size laws in each dimension

Location: line 111, anchor `sec:PLACE-SIZE-LAW`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 113
Closing passage line: 204

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p113 sha256 1901fc4e63d3a5b50382c5e7752d17da5da6af0c255516e359814c81b92aeae6 -->
p113, lines 113-113; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p108; next: p115

> A round region's radius and its volume have different units and different distributions. This geometric conversion explains why the model predicts different histograms in a track, an arena, and a room.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p115 sha256 9c8a12ab71aff30a3dc04fceee8cd369033919ef749dd4e1871a394025152900 -->
p115, lines 115-115; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p113; next: p117

> Let $v_d=\pi^{d/2}/\Gamma(1+d/2)$ be the volume of a unit ball in $d$ dimensions. For $d=1,2,3$, it is $2,\pi,4\pi/3$. [The symbol $\Gamma$ is the [**gamma function**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, defined for $t>0$ by $\Gamma(t)=\int_0^\infty u^{t-1}e^{-u}\,du$. It extends factorials: $\Gamma(n+1)=n!$.]{.definition-explanation}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p117 sha256 2622397a97e4137834c9aecff9dee82065045328ad11e47745542a699a321117 -->
p117, lines 117-117; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p115; next: p126

p126, lines 126-126; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p117; next: p128

> This is the paper's Equation 1. The expression for $\beta$ above is its leading high threshold geometric scale, not an exact finite threshold identity. A finite threshold simulation can share the approximate shape while having a different fitted scale.[^p3-size-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p147 sha256 70e635652302213a2cf731e3084b884490fbb2f91b7de49476aeffc988347e81 -->
p147, lines 147-147; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p145; next: p156

> The mean and its corresponding scale are

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p156 sha256 40466c8bfdaa55ee7d964ad00a2b7b86b46bf68fd08af4d6a474b429e4080065 -->
p156, lines 156-156; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p147; next: p166

> The same substitution verifies normalization: $\int_0^\infty p_d(s)\,ds=\int_0^\infty e^{-u}\,du=1$. It also supplies every positive moment,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p166 sha256 e0cefd2bef21d8c57c06ba03819d1f74f6d0f41e3ad03b4ed921afc4a741b404 -->
p166, lines 166-166; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p156; next: p168

> Size has units of distance to the power $d$, while $\beta$ has units of inverse distance squared in every dimension. This makes $\beta s^{2/d}$ dimensionless, and makes $p_d(s)$ a density per unit size. These checks catch mistakes in changing variables.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p168 sha256 5eb4bde29d56abc33274fa983b0769051b44b928b70a23d40313217e78de35aa -->
p168, lines 168-168; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p166; next: p170

> Writing dimensionless size as $w=\beta^{d/2}s$ removes the size scale. The dimensional size laws then differ in shape, while the transformed variable $U=\beta s^{2/d}=w^{2/d}$ has the same exponential law.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p170 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p170, lines 170-170; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p168; next: p174

p176, lines 176-176; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p174; next: p178

> Removing units does not erase the difference between length, area, and volume distributions. The nonlinear transformation to U removes that shape difference. Its shared exponential law is the assumption used in the change of variables. See [Field-size laws, Eq. 1, p. 1111](../papers/mainali-2025-universal-statistics.pdf#page=3).

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p178 sha256 9960784fd6b96a12fbf5b8855619bf1a26dae079bce219c3b96e0c64ec075ad2 -->
p178, lines 178-178; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p176; next: p182

p182, lines 182-182; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p178; next: p184

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p184 sha256 2b42174fb3aa8cdb367dbafaf20beebf72289607eaee370aa6fdbb44762ef886 -->
p184, lines 184-186; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p182; next: p188

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = NO --> [A [**Weibull distribution**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} has survival probability $\exp[-(s/b)^k]$ for size $s>0$, scale $b>0$, and shape $k>0$.]{.definition-explanation} Here $k=2/d$ and $b=\beta^{-d/2}$. In 3D the diverging density does not mean a positive probability of exactly zero volume. The probability of $0<s<\delta$ still tends to zero as $\delta\to0$. <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p188 sha256 1198b5a52fcf5236f4556770b0651c726dee0edcbce5726be8f473d67ea222db -->
p188, lines 188-188; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p184; next: p190

> For a concrete comparison, set $\sigma=1$ m and $q=5$ in this limiting calculation. The mean size is $0.5013$ m in 1D, $0.2513$ m$^2$ in 2D, and $0.1260$ m$^3$ in 3D. These values describe the same local distance scale and threshold, rather than fields with the same numerical size in different units. More generally, the geometry and moment formula simplify to $\mu_s\simeq(\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma/q)^d$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p190 sha256 6b7824f01bb842b0615cc5a043713743572a8ed808f415513a4f015a335755db -->
p190, lines 190-194; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p188; next: p198

> <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerLeads = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> This approximation has a measurable error even for a smooth Gaussian process. At $q=1.1,\sigma=2.4$ m, its 1D mean is $5.469$ m, compared with the [exact stationary crossing result](ch-place-fields.html#sec-place-inference) $3.746$ m, an overestimate of $46.0\%$. At $q=5$ the error is $3.73\%$. [This is why the paper compares data to finite threshold simulations as well as to limiting curves.]{.key-statement} <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerLeads = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p198 sha256 ff864c1077a2589498fd3bf05187ff3d7e4d9a4009d33a0cfb023d7fb36e0d27 -->
p198, lines 198-198; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p190; next: p200

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p200 sha256 916af41df8e7a07fcadee25de0f885c71ee63808c3a11e063878ac3469d10f1b -->
p200, lines 200-200; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p198; next: p201

> Equation S18 prints multiplication by mean size where the preceding moment formula requires division.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p201 sha256 cbdcb17a297de8911bad66e90999d69ea4f98394709189fef43cfa0b609e7be5 -->
p201, lines 201-201; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p200; next: p202

> Equation S23 omits a factor $\sqrt2$ when solving the parabola in S22 for full width.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p202 sha256 6e39c1527f2b69e4322110f808587d200d1f28916006e58fe58cf3424b5662ba -->
p202, lines 202-202; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p201; next: p204

> In S25 the finite-height quadratic exponent should be negative.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p204 sha256 deac0912bfaf09f16cc10a216a3b029614dd70ca334a957405101ead113e3aff -->
p204, lines 204-204; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p202; next: p210

These corrections concern the printed derivation. They do not establish that the simulation code or empirical fits used the same mistakes.[^p3-corrections]

- Claim c23, line 180: "| Space: $d=2$ | Meaning of $s$: area | Density: $\beta e^{-\beta s}$ | Consequence: Exponential. Mean area is $1/\beta$. |" -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c24, line 180: "| Space: $d=3$ | Meaning of $s$: volume | Density: $(2\beta/3)s^{-1/3}e^{-\beta s^{2/3}}$ | Consequence: Weibull with shape $2/3$. Density rises without bound near zero but is integrable. |" -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05b-place-sizes.md::sec:PLACE-SLICES -->

### Slices test spatial structure beyond field volume

Location: line 208, anchor `sec:PLACE-SLICES`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 210
Closing passage line: 252

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p210 sha256 f7a4d1df563695a6f456b6601656f1ad5be30d37c443f44aea6f10e8f88cc427 -->
p210, lines 210-210; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p204; next: p212

> A model can fit field volumes and still place activity incorrectly within those volumes. Slicing the map tests that internal spatial structure.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p212 sha256 1a2f62a3f077027c6bc052dc3e82860628a0f997969929fd9e093cdf68b74628 -->
p212, lines 212-214; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p210; next: p216

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> Restrict a Gaussian field to a straight line $x=x_0+tv$. At any chosen values $t_1,\ldots,t_n$, its values are a subset of the original jointly Gaussian values. They therefore remain jointly Gaussian. Along the line the covariance is $C((t-t')v)$. For a unit direction in an isotropic field it has the same local length scale $\sigma$. The same argument applies to a flat plane. First slicing and then thresholding gives the same active portions as thresholding and then slicing.[^p3-slices-source] <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p216 sha256 f2d3b34edd97515ee6dbcf06364aa190610fce5130a06f53795e78ab8bb662da -->
p216, lines 216-220; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p212; next: p222

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO --> Thus a line through a 2D or 3D map has the one dimensional excursion prediction, and a plane through a 3D map has the two dimensional prediction. The field being counted is now a connected active interval or patch in that slice. One 3D component can be missed, cut into multiple pieces, or intersect several slices. <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p222 sha256 82a87cc1992fc3598829c339d624d666eb0a12b2f845175d481bcda2521f1f14 -->
p222, lines 222-222; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p216; next: p224

> Choosing one original field uniformly and cutting through its centre is different from choosing lines uniformly through space. Large fields are more likely to be hit by a spatially sampled line, and most hits pass away from their centres. The Gaussian restriction argument describes excursions along the sampled line. The radius distribution for fields counted once each cannot be used directly as the distribution of lengths observed along a line. In the high peak picture, an isotropic $d$ dimensional field has a radius of order $\sigma/q$. Converting this same radius into $d$ dimensional size and then cutting it requires both hit probability and cut position.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p224 sha256 dcbb4960853c0a7ca89e7d923d8f592935f10cada772e0a1560dac9b68666ed7 -->
p224, lines 224-224; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p222; next: p226

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05b-place-sizes.md::sec:PLACE-SLOPES -->

### Why slopes at field boundaries are Rayleigh

Location: line 255, anchor `sec:PLACE-SLOPES`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 257
Closing passage line: 320

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p257 sha256 e344c1bebf0db137fc241848261e512767ee4d9a2a050e602e340fdef310b0a0 -->
p257, lines 257-257; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p252; next: p259

> The derivative at an arbitrary position is Gaussian. A field boundary is a selected position, however, and selecting it changes the derivative distribution.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p259 sha256 fb4d93baeb2d07a0468341c3f611f160bb689ff9747952eaaad5691a739b2303 -->
p259, lines 259-259; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p257; next: p267

> Return to the [crossing integral](ch-place-fields.html#sec-place-kac-rice) and retain only slopes between $v$ and $v+dv$. Their contribution is proportional to $v p_{z'}(v)dv$. Dividing by the total contribution of positive slopes gives the slope density at a typical upcrossing:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p267 sha256 9e2e7e48c3c91083439e24344b698a1c2786947f15f23d9898f5b596536693f7 -->
p267, lines 267-269; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p259; next: p273

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> The height density cancels because height and slope are independent. [This Rayleigh law is therefore independent of $q$ under the smooth stationary Gaussian model, without a high threshold approximation.]{.key-statement} Absolute slopes at downward boundaries have the same law. The rate slope is $av$, so its scale also depends on the firing rate gain. This selection effect explains why a half Gaussian law for a positive derivative sampled uniformly in space would be wrong at crossings.[^p3-slope-source] <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p273 sha256 65eb0b476fa28ef5ac949160ef950c255718a10344956fe5e81ac9a5dd55fc0a -->
p273, lines 273-273; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p267; next: p279

> There is also an independent check on the high threshold width constant. Starting at an upcrossing with slope $v$, the local excursion is approximately

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p279 sha256 8a1dc3346431d47dcdafec9e28737546824f0fb1d2eb9dfcbcabc9771538b7a2 -->
p279, lines 279-279; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p273; next: p283

> Its second root is $s=2\sigma^2v/q$. Changing variables in the exact crossing slope law gives $p(s)=2\beta s e^{-\beta s^2}$ with $\beta=q^2/(8\sigma^2)$, the same constant as the peak calculation for $v_1=2$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p283 sha256 b4aa0a8bba754e076f31636fdebd2a9fc4be1256567358656d7739816d44dc7c -->
p283, lines 283-285; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p279; next: p287

> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO --> Figure 3A identifies which width, peak, and slopes are measured. B compares local peak counts, C–D compare rate and size, and E compares boundary slopes. The paper measures boundary slopes by a finite difference between samples 0.1 m apart. This approximates a derivative and has its own spatial resolution. It is not a direct observation of the underlying synaptic input. <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p287 sha256 7dd8a49d1fc85b0197ccf6145d2da2ee11e06a3e5e746bfc6a03b9a2cd22a5c5 -->
p287, lines 287-289; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p283; next: p293

> </div> </details>

## ch:PLACE-TESTS - Holes and measurements test the spatial explanation

Source file: `content/chapters/05c-place-tests.md`

Purpose: Recordings show where a hippocampal cell is active as an animal moves through an environment. This chapter tests the spatial model against the shapes and arrangement of those regions, then asks what agreement can reveal about the inputs and connections that produce them.

Prerequisites: ch:PLACE-FIELDS, ch:PLACE-SIZES

Sidecar sections: Holes distinguish maps with the same field count, What was fitted and what was tested, Random input weights can generate Gaussian maps

Blocks: 6 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p3 sha256 21a8bd7ecc496fe03e6e7765910d3c3bbdbfae8b0e5894aebf654665abaf45cc -->
p3, lines 3-5; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: none; next: p7

> <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO --> [Two maps can have the same field sizes and counts while arranging their active regions differently. One may contain rings or tunnels that the other lacks. Can the Gaussian spatial model account for these differences, and how much would that agreement tell us about the circuit that produced the maps?]{.key-statement} <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p7 sha256 2e3309c33081b2eaac4e0945b5a0ed403df1d85e2d55ca39616ed4ab8c71551c -->
p7, lines 7-7; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p3; next: p9

> We first count connected regions and holes together, which gives a prediction across activity thresholds. The boundary of the observed environment matters for this calculation, so we keep its contributions. We then examine which measured quantities set the model's parameters and which provide further tests. Finally, a sum of many randomly weighted inputs shows one way a circuit could produce Gaussian spatial input, while making clear which assumptions and circuit measurements that explanation still needs.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p9 sha256 2b3079f6c4e66ebc997f03c60b0ed38aeedcdf7e1a22260f159d9b11d7693ff6 -->
p9, lines 9-9; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p7; next: p13

> The map is still fixed over space. As before, $q$ is the normalized activity threshold, $\sigma$ is the local spatial scale, and $a$ converts normalized input above threshold into firing rate. The number of spatial dimensions is $d$. It is separate from the diffusion coefficient $D$ used in the memory chapters.

### Holes distinguish maps with the same field count

Location: line 11, anchor `sec:PLACE-TOPOLOGY`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 13
Closing passage line: 139

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p13 sha256 5840aa382ec860103f4d1b060206810b25bca00324c3860f78ca1ecd3b758dce -->
p13, lines 13-15; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p9; next: p17

> <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO --> Two maps can have the same field count and total active area but different holes. As the activity threshold rises, active regions shrink and can split or disappear. The numbers of components and holes can change. <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p17 sha256 0c2ab7e80f3619baf767872884b081ee44ec7f72e71df3bb2e4ec839a1725abb -->
p17, lines 17-17; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p13; next: p19

> [The [**Euler characteristic**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, written $\chi$, combines these counts differently in each dimension:]{.definition-explanation}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p19 sha256 98c6562e449ebfc975a7417e7a2310b1e7c6c3c88872a75310c3272aba5bde57 -->
p19, lines 19-20; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p17; next: p21

> In 1D it equals the number of connected active intervals. <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p21 sha256 4ac745f2399363962823df78f4b16ddcebd071c3a620e042d51840996f7203bb -->
p21, lines 21-22; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p19; next: p23

> In 2D it is the number of connected components minus the number of independent holes. Three disjoint disks have $\chi=3$. One connected ring has $\chi=1-1=0$. <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p23 sha256 f6c9a4588ef99b3239033d5ecc56a4ee4f69ab2872575aad354694ebdd772e19 -->
p23, lines 23-23; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p21; next: p25

> In 3D it is components minus independent tunnels plus enclosed cavities. A solid ball has $\chi=1$, a solid ring has $\chi=0$, and a hollow spherical shell has $\chi=2$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p25 sha256 17b85e25c1216cbb4b2f7910697ec49a04db8157c0b0dd6361ff5955092006a8 -->
p25, lines 25-25; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p23; next: p27

> [These counts are the first three [**Betti numbers**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}: numbers of independent components, loops, and cavities, respectively.]{.definition-explanation}[^p3-ec-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p27 sha256 f0d912c242f1c3298afc120785c32067c289e55b31ec386020154b4944f6df02 -->
p27, lines 27-27; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p25; next: p29

> Thresholding one fixed smooth map separates the number of connected fields from the number of holes.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p29 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p29, lines 29-29; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p27; next: p33

> <div class="figure-scroll">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p33 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p33, lines 33-33; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p29; next: p35

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p35 sha256 be79eb69f6109d899f86e8bac03bee74dbf19681aa49cf26f8bc258eee48b58a -->
p35, lines 35-35; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p33; next: p37

> A negative chi means that holes outnumber components in this 2D example. It does not mean a negative number of fields. When holes are absent, chi equals the component count.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p37 sha256 65d3fcc7e31987c23e572fdf0fe843f734103ab91aafa1e3432eec27b9786811 -->
p37, lines 37-37; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p35; next: p39

> The expected Euler characteristic has an exact Gaussian formula even where identifying it with field count would be wrong. We already derived the 1D case. To understand the higher dimensional factors, we use a mathematical result: [for a smooth function on a compact domain whose [**critical point**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}s have zero gradient and invertible Hessians, the Euler characteristic of a level set region can be computed as a signed count of critical points, including boundary contributions. This is the relevant conclusion of [**Morse theory**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}.]{.definition-explanation} We do not prove that topological theorem here. The calculation below shows how its Gaussian expectation produces the paper's polynomials and boundary terms. The general Gaussian result is given by Taylor's kinematic formula.[^p3-taylor]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p39 sha256 b92ab15eb79ba09c98042d433b3da516d4c8d039db5489694bc80b6033e2b9d4 -->
p39, lines 39-41; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p37; next: p49

> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO --> First take the interior of a $j$ dimensional region, with $j\le3$, and set $\lambda=\sigma^{-2}$. A critical point has gradient zero. The multidimensional Kac–Rice rule counts these zeros with the absolute Hessian determinant. The Morse signs replace that absolute determinant by $\det(-H)$. The resulting signed density above level $q$ is <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p49 sha256 a54adb9dc66647dc11a47e1a99103408b5b1516b6a0959b97f26244ec1647232 -->
p49, lines 49-49; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p39; next: p51

> where $\phi(b)=e^{-b^2/2}/\sqrt{2\pi}$. The gradient's $j$ components are independent Gaussians of variance $\lambda$, so $p_{\nabla z}(0)=(2\pi\lambda)^{-j/2}$. The determinant measures how spatial volume near a gradient zero converts to gradient volume, just as the slope did for a scalar crossing.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p51 sha256 710dd87df9b25e8475fe88f2037ff2191ed664f4e4b000fe059c857b143b6732 -->
p51, lines 51-51; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p49; next: p59

> The resulting signed densities are

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p59 sha256 a7b5eaf9118abeb9aa49bb2332a925d2d12b0f8fadb10cec749495df1f5cc4a1 -->
p59, lines 59-59; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p51; next: p63

> These are signed densities per length, area, and volume, not three formulas for positive field counts.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p63 sha256 d44c31426f5895ab37596805d6891caf90e79013d4d00d0964a4c8dd03a9ba3e -->
p63, lines 63-63; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p59; next: p71

> Write the conditional Hessian as $H=-b\lambda I+G$, where $G$ has zero mean. Differentiating an isotropic covariance four times gives, for distinct indices $i,k$,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p71 sha256 1f5731c4dc8f1b826c49926ad97d341e17ad2a85697d5db14245583ec43348b6 -->
p71, lines 71-73; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p63; next: p79

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> The constant $\kappa$ describes a fourth derivative of covariance. Conditioning on height subtracts $\lambda^2$ from the covariance of the two diagonal entries, but not from the off-diagonal variance. Hence each pair contributes <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p79 sha256 cb8ee9c23b0a54dd3f61d7f86bec7f1e1c13bb46f74f32a40a2dce842745c7c4 -->
p79, lines 79-81; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p71; next: p83

> <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO --> For $j=1$, $\mathbb E[\det(-H)\mid b]=b\lambda$. For $j=2$, expand the two by two determinant to obtain $\lambda^2(b^2-1)$. For $j=3$, the leading term is $b^3\lambda^3$. The three index pairs each contribute $-b\lambda^3$, and all odd centered Gaussian products vanish. The result is $\lambda^3(b^3-3b)$. The unknown fourth derivative $\kappa$ has canceled. <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p83 sha256 ccb3f22095106d1560d4d4abd636f04939cba9b69067374fb410037ad38bb37d -->
p83, lines 83-83; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p79; next: p93

> Integrating the three polynomials gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p93 sha256 759ae33c6e6f42a13d8c606872b897cd45dd89da247e86535518830ce9f8c227 -->
p93, lines 93-95; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p83; next: p99

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> For example, differentiate $-q\phi(q)$ to get $(q^2-1)\phi(q)$, which verifies the second integral using its vanishing upper endpoint. Combining the determinant, gradient density, and integral gives the signed densities above. <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p99 sha256 519c9977d8d77cc263e3024f23ca7da7d5e0d6185ab85315daee51c4bf58b341 -->
p99, lines 99-101; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p93; next: p114

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> Now include the boundary of an ordinary closed square or cube of side $L$, whose opposite faces are not joined and with every intersecting component retained. On a $j$ dimensional face, derivatives along the face give the same calculation as above. Each of its $d-j$ gradient components perpendicular to the face and directed out of the box must have the appropriate sign to contribute to the boundary Morse count. Symmetry and independence give a factor $2^{-(d-j)}$. There are $2^{d-j}\binom dj$ such faces, each of size $L^j$. Their combined coefficient is therefore $\binom djL^j$. Corners supply $Q(q)$ in total. With $r=L/\sigma$, the formulas become <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p114 sha256 d97e37b9fc87cf5d332ffd4fdcbbc3a6ce6b55f1c724bf34337353601f0da221 -->
p114, lines 114-116; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p99; next: p118

> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO --> These reproduce the paper's Equations 8–10 for interval, square, and cube geometry. They are not the formulas for a periodic box. For a rectangle or rectangular room, replace $2L$ or $3L$ by the sum of side lengths, $3L^2$ by the sum of pairwise side products, and $L^3$ by volume. A general domain requires its own geometric boundary factors. Applying only the volume term to a small room would discard real boundary contributions. <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

p121, lines 121-121; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p120; next: p123

p123, lines 123-127; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p121; next: p129

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.OverusedVocabulary = NO --> At intermediate thresholds the higher dimensional signed densities can be negative because tunnels or holes outnumber components. For example a cube with $L/\sigma=10$ has expected $\chi=-20.06$ at $q=0$. A negative expected Euler characteristic is meaningful. A negative expected field count would not be. <!-- vale ai-tells.OverusedVocabulary = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p129 sha256 b19a80efc6ef3cebb84771df7a93a897523e424ec20f0935d3698bb0248e429e -->
p129, lines 129-129; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p123; next: p137

> At a sufficiently high threshold, almost every component is a small solid blob with one peak and no holes. Then $\chi\simeq N$. For a large box, retaining the leading volume term and the leading power of $q$ gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p137 sha256 3e99e169f76da7d6308d00454de6b147ec2d3c69aa0157b1402b9dfe2afce696 -->
p137, lines 137-137; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p129; next: p139

> where $V$ is environment length, area, or volume. Dividing $VQ(q)$ by this count and using $Q(q)\simeq\phi(q)/q$ recovers $\mu_s\simeq(\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma/q)^d$, independently checking the size-law mean. [The exact EC formula and this approximate component count have different ranges of validity.]{.key-statement}[^p3-higher-count]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p139 sha256 f0f6158c6556ca486bd2eac4b7cc7de815509606f68c1f5e63abe19cec7aad85 -->
p139, lines 139-143; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p137; next: p147

> <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO --> Read Figure 3F–H as whole curves, rather than single field counts. Increasing the applied firing rate cutoff by $t\ge0$ changes the normalized level to $q+t/a$. It can reveal separation between peaks or remove components. The measured maps only test levels at or above their original effective cutoff. The rest of an analytic curve is a prediction. The gain was matched using mean maximum firing rate before the EC comparison, so the horizontal conversion from Hz to normalized level also uses fitted information. Two dimensional holes and three dimensional tunnels help explain the nonmonotonic curves.[^p3-ec-source] <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->

For the concrete-wording field, use this form: line N; phrase: ...; paraphrase: ...; actor: ...; object: ...; action: ...; condition or consequence: ...; verdict: retain, revise, or delete; replacement or retention reason: ....
Context review must explain the literal meaning, prerequisites, analogy limits when relevant, and reason for appearing here. If no correction is needed, give a specific retention reason; lint success is not evidence.
For System setup and component relationships, cite the passage and fill: setting: ...; roles: ...; objects and flow: ...; assumptions: ...; verdict: ....
For Relevant alternatives, common criteria, and selection or scope, cite the passage and fill: common problem: ...; options: ...; relationship: ...; criteria: ...; selection or scope: ...; verdict: ....
For Essential explanation beyond the checklist, cite the passage and fill: reader task: ...; dependencies checked: ...; reasoning chain: ...; unresolved: ....
For Expert objection, cite the passage and fill: objection: ...; kind: ...; target: ...; disposition: ...; reason: ....
For system setup and alternatives, an adequate review uses verdict: adequate. Where inapplicable, use Not applicable: line N; reason: <specific reader-benefit reason>.
Expert objection cannot be marked Not applicable. Write the strongest correction a domain expert would make to this unit's text or figures; set kind to correctness or wording; name its target passage or figure label; set disposition to fixed or does not apply; give the reason. An objection of none is not a review. The strongest correction is the one that would change what a reader believes or does. Aim first at what the unit claims: a promise word such as always, once, ensures or remains that is false or needs a condition; a named method described wrongly; code that does not do what the text says; a table cell; a diagnostic sequence that skips a stage; a verb without an actor; a label naming something other than what is drawn; a figure that disagrees with the text or another figure; and example content found in neither the chapter nor its sources. A unit whose claim-bearing content includes code, a table, an equation, or a figure needs a correctness objection; a wording objection alone does not count there.
Claims to check lists the unit's statements most likely to make checkable claims: every table row and every figure or table caption with its alt text, and each sentence with a promise word, a quantity or calculation, code font, a citation, or a section or figure reference. Footnote definitions are left to the citation checks. When packets compare with a revision, every changed statement is listed first and marked changed. For each claim, set verdict to true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim, and after checked: say how it was checked, such as the code test, the recomputation, or the source and its location. The list is a floor, not the extent of the review.
For each code listing, write test: followed by the path, relative to the guide, of a test that runs it on the inputs the text makes claims about, or not run: followed by the reason and how its claims were checked instead.
Essential explanation cannot be marked Not applicable. Trace the reader's task and its dependencies, including gaps not covered by other fields. Record unresolved: none only after resolving essential gaps; otherwise describe them and keep the unit verdict open. A complete checklist is not proof of complete teaching.

p147, lines 147-149; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p139; next: p151

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> The experiments supply estimated firing rate maps, not the hidden Gaussian input. To assess a prediction, we must therefore track both which summaries set its parameters and which processing decisions change the map's geometry. <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p151 sha256 c5cffb8cd55627e0055bf97aa1844e152b2c8ab1a579ef07866024cfa752b864 -->

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO --> The paper reanalyzes earlier bat and rodent recordings. It uses different available summaries in different conditions. The inspected article and supplement do not clearly give the final included cell count for every analysis, particularly the long-tunnel and 3D reanalyses. Counts from an original recording study should therefore not be silently substituted for those final samples.[^p3-processing] <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

> The STAR Methods call the virtual-track animals rats in one sentence. Figure 1F and the main results identify them as mice. Several STAR figure cross-references also appear displaced. The table follows the main figure captions and the analyses described in full, rather than those isolated labels.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p165 sha256 b1a1e46bb9e32f238aa6e77d3fb7cf7ca40135ef6e5595d05d3f74c8b2f64f73 -->
p165, lines 165-167; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p163; next: p169

> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> Figure 1C distinguishes a limiting analytical Rayleigh curve from the finite threshold simulation histogram. Its parameters were constrained by both mean length and mean gap, so the full length histogram is an additional check. Figure 1D does the same for gaps. E tests skew and excess kurtosis of log lengths. For $Y=\log s$, write $m_k=\mathbb E[(Y-\mathbb EY)^k]$. [Then [**skew**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is $m_3/m_2^{3/2}$ and [**excess kurtosis**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is $m_4/m_2^2-3$. A [**lognormal distribution**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} of sizes makes $Y$ Gaussian and both population quantities zero.]{.definition-explanation} Finite sample estimates still fluctuate, which is why the figure compares simulated sampling distributions.[^p3-fit-tests] <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p169 sha256 80c133233f0ca00a798bc0ad26e696d112785a848e7f266a0106d4c5e0ef9453 -->
p169, lines 169-169; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p165; next: p171

> For the long-tunnel data, the authors compared the Gaussian excursion model with a fitted lognormal using likelihoods and criteria that penalize fitted parameter count. Those comparisons favored the Gaussian model. [These are in-sample model comparisons. They are not reported held-out forecasts.]{.key-statement} Model histogram error bars come from $10^4$ simulations matched to the number of recorded cells. They do not quantify every source of animal-level or measurement uncertainty.[^p3-fit-tests]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p183 sha256 f95906b7ae3274e781311b8fbc7a559461afaa554bf3618683f183c2097fa851 -->
p183, lines 183-183; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p179; next: p185

> Figure S1 matches the rat maze's 15 cm minimum length cutoff in simulations and removes unusually large empirical fields. Changing that cutoff changes log-length moments. Figure S5 shows sensitivity of the higher-moment comparison across small denoising thresholds, while the rate versus width slope becomes stable above about 1 Hz. Figure S2 compares the spherical-field alternative and measures boundary curvature. [Locally, [**mean curvature**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} averages bending in two principal directions and [**Gaussian curvature**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} multiplies them: a spherical patch bends the same way in both directions, while a saddle bends in opposite directions.]{.definition-explanation} These are extra shape measurements. They are not needed to derive the field-size law. Surface representations and finite differences affect their estimates.[^p3-supp-tests]

> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO --> <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO --> Figure 4A–B summarizes fitted parameter changes across a small set of environments. The correlation length increases with environment size while the threshold generally decreases. Increasing $\sigma$ reduces crossings per metre, while decreasing $q$ increases them. Both parameters also affect mean length, so these effects must be considered together. Figure S3 reports count scaling exponents below one and hence a weak decline in count per unit environment size. These cross-experiment trends do not establish a biological scaling law. In particular, the 3D table's reported $\sigma=4.0$ has an unclear distance-unit conversion relative to the spatial binning, so it should not be read as a verified value in metres.[^p3-trends] <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES --> <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

> <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO --> The model also allows differences between cells. Figure S4 draws a positive threshold separately for each cell and keeps that threshold for two otherwise independent maps representing opposite flight directions. Low-threshold cells tend to have more and larger fields in both maps. This shared cell property creates correlations between flight directions even when field locations differ between directions. Two distribution parameters were chosen to match the two empirical correlations, so those correlations are fitted features of this extension. Its continued agreement with length and gap distributions is the additional comparison. This extension should not be described as a two-parameter model shared by all cells.[^p3-threshold-variation] <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->

For System setup and component relationships, cite the passage and fill: setting: ...; roles: ...; objects and flow: ...; assumptions: ...; verdict: ....
For Relevant alternatives, common criteria, and selection or scope, cite the passage and fill: common problem: ...; options: ...; relationship: ...; criteria: ...; selection or scope: ...; verdict: ....
For Essential explanation beyond the checklist, cite the passage and fill: reader task: ...; dependencies checked: ...; reasoning chain: ...; unresolved: ....

For system setup and alternatives, an adequate review uses verdict: adequate. Where inapplicable, use Not applicable: line N; reason: <specific reader-benefit reason>.
Expert objection cannot be marked Not applicable. Write the strongest correction a domain expert would make to this unit's text or figures; set kind to correctness or wording; name its target passage or figure label; set disposition to fixed or does not apply; give the reason. An objection of none is not a review. The strongest correction is the one that would change what a reader believes or does. Aim first at what the unit claims: a promise word such as always, once, ensures or remains that is false or needs a condition; a named method described wrongly; code that does not do what the text says; a table cell; a diagnostic sequence that skips a stage; a verb without an actor; a label naming something other than what is drawn; a figure that disagrees with the text or another figure; and example content found in neither the chapter nor its sources. A unit whose claim-bearing content includes code, a table, an equation, or a figure needs a correctness objection; a wording objection alone does not count there.
Claims to check lists the unit's statements most likely to make checkable claims: every table row and every figure or table caption with its alt text, and each sentence with a promise word, a quantity or calculation, code font, a citation, or a section or figure reference. Footnote definitions are left to the citation checks. When packets compare with a revision, every changed statement is listed first and marked changed. For each claim, set verdict to true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim, and after checked: say how it was checked, such as the code test, the recomputation, or the source and its location. The list is a floor, not the extent of the review.
For each code listing, write test: followed by the path, relative to the guide, of a test that runs it on the inputs the text makes claims about, or not run: followed by the reason and how its claims were checked instead.
Essential explanation cannot be marked Not applicable. Trace the reader's task and its dependencies, including gaps not covered by other fields. Record unresolved: none only after resolving essential gaps; otherwise describe them and keep the unit verdict open. A complete checklist is not proof of complete teaching.
